  - [16:08 - 16:11] **Introduction to Data Modification and Input Noise**

    Okay, so we just established that early stopping is mathematically equivalent to L2 regularization if we modify the distance metric to be measured from the initial condition. Now, let us look at the final regularization method for today before we jump back into the code demo. The final method we want to talk about is explicitly modifying the data itself by adding noise. Crucially, we only inject this noise during the training phase, not during inference. Why would you want to intentionally add noise to your perfectly good data? The fundamental rationale is that you want to force your model to be robust to noise and slight variations in the real world. If you hold this belief that the underlying function should be smooth, you can consider adding noise to the data to actively enforce it. When we say "add noise," we can actually mean several different things depending on where exactly we inject it. You could add noise directly to the input data features, you could add it to the outputs or labels, or you could even add it directly to the network's internal weights. Adding noise in these different locations will result in fundamentally different implicit priors for the resulting trained model. Perhaps the simplest one to intuitively understand is adding noise to the inputs, and this is the specific variant we will analyze in deep mathematical detail. Adding noise to the input data is heuristically equivalent to placing a prior that the model's output should remain highly insensitive to random, small perturbations of the input space. You can think of this as a form of translation invariance, but specifically invariant to small localized pixel or feature perturbations. If you add a tiny bit of random noise to an image, the model's output prediction absolutely should not change. For example, if you look at the slide, we have a clean image of a handwritten digit 7 from the MNIST dataset. If we systematically add an increasing amount of random Gaussian noise to those pixels, your human eyes can still easily tell that it is the number 7, and the underlying label should definitively not change. We want our neural network to possess that exact same robust capability, and we achieve that by explicitly training it on these artificially noisy examples.  

    好的，我们刚刚确定了，如果我们修改距离度量使其从初始条件开始测量，提前停止在数学上就等同于L2正则化。现在，在我们跳回到代码演示之前，让我们看看今天的最后一种正则化方法。我们要讨论的最后一种方法是通过添加噪声来显式地修改数据本身。至关重要的是，我们只在训练阶段注入这种噪声，而不是在推理阶段。你为什么要有意地向你完美的数据中添加噪声呢？根本的理由是你希望迫使你的模型对现实世界中的噪声和轻微变化具有稳健性。如果你确信底层函数应该是平滑的，你可以考虑向数据中添加噪声来主动强制实现这一点。当我们说“添加噪声”时，根据我们具体注入的位置，实际上可以有几种不同的含义。你可以直接向输入数据特征添加噪声，可以向输出或标签添加噪声，甚至可以直接向网络的内部权重添加噪声。在这些不同位置添加噪声将为最终训练出的模型带来根本不同的隐式先验。也许最容易直观理解的是向输入添加噪声，这也是我们将进行深入数学分析的特定变体。向输入数据添加噪声，在启发式上等同于设置了一个先验，即模型的输出应保持对输入空间的随机、微小扰动极度不敏感。你可以把它看作是平移不变性的一种形式，但具体来说是对微小的局部像素或特征扰动具有不变性。如果你给一张图片添加一点点随机噪声，模型的输出预测绝对不应该改变。例如，看幻灯片，我们有一张来自MNIST数据集的清晰的手写数字7的图像。如果我们系统地向这些像素添加越来越多的随机高斯噪声，你的人眼仍然可以很容易地辨认出那是数字7，且底层标签绝对不应该改变。我们希望我们的神经网络具备完全相同的稳健能力，我们通过在这些人为添加噪声的样本上对其进行显式训练来实现这一点。  

  - [16:11 - 16:22] **Mathematical Analysis of Input Noise in Linear Regression**

    Let us analyze exactly what happens mathematically when you add noise to the inputs, using our familiar linear regression example to keep the algebra tractable. The original objective function in linear regression is the standard least squares empirical risk: R(w) = 1/2 ||Xw - y||^2. Now, suppose we strictly add noise directly to the input matrix X. The input X becomes a corrupted matrix X_tilde, defined mathematically as X_tilde = X + Z. What exactly is this noise matrix Z? Since our pristine dataset matrix X has N rows (for N distinct samples) and M columns (for M distinct features), we can think of the matrix Z as having the exact same dimensions. Each row of Z is the specific noise vector added to the i-th training sample. We mandate that these noise vectors are independently and identically distributed (IID) drawn from a normal distribution centered precisely at zero, with a covariance matrix of δ * I, where δ is some small positive scalar representing the variance. Essentially, we are adding isotropic Gaussian noise uniformly across all features. We want to rigidly analyze how this injection changes the optimization objective and the resulting learned parameters. Our modified, noise-regularized objective becomes R_tilde(w) = 1/2 ||X_tilde w - y||^2. Substituting our noisy definition, this evaluates to 1/2 ||(X + Z)w - y||^2. If we algebraically expand this squared norm, we are left with three distinct terms: the original uncorrupted risk 1/2 ||Xw - y||^2, a pure noise penalty term 1/2 ||Zw||^2, and a cross-correlation term (Zw)^T (Xw - y). Because our noise matrix Z is inherently a random variable, we must evaluate the expected value of this modified risk, denoted as E[R_tilde(w)]. Since statistical expectation is a linear operator, we can seamlessly distribute it to each term individually. The expectation of the cross-term gracefully collapses to zero because the noise Z has a mean of exactly zero. Thus, we uniquely only need to evaluate the expectation of the middle term: E[1/2 ||Zw||^2]. Let's write it out concisely to spot the pattern. The squared norm ||Zw||^2 is fundamentally just the sum over all N samples of the squared dot product between the i-th noise row vector z_i and the model weight vector w. So we have 1/2 * sum( (z_i * w)^2 ). Dragging the expectation operator strictly inside the sum, we look closely at E[(z_i * w)^2]. Because z_i is a normally distributed random vector with a diagonal covariance of δ * I, the expectation of this squared projection simply evaluates mathematically to δ * ||w||^2. Since we are summing this identical expected value identically over all N samples, the total expectation of the pure noise term neatly becomes 1/2 * N * δ * ||w||^2. Therefore, the expected value of our modified objective is exactly equal to the original empirical objective R(w) plus a strict penalty term: 1/2 * (N * δ) * ||w||^2. Does this structural shape look familiar? In expectation, dynamically injecting Gaussian noise into the inputs of a linear regression model is mathematically identical to applying standard analytical L2 regularization, where the equivalent regularization strength α is simply N times δ. So it seems we have the exact same story repeating over and over again! In the linear realm, whether we explicitly use L2 penalties, implement early stopping, or randomly inject input noise, they incredibly all algebraically collapse into equivalent forms of L2 weight decay. Of course, always explicitly remember that this perfect mathematical equivalence only holds true for purely linear models; highly nonlinear neural networks are not mathematically guaranteed to behave identically, though their macroscopic regularizing effects remain highly similar.  

    让我们确切地分析一下向输入添加噪声时在数学上会发生什么，为了让代数计算易于处理，我们使用我们熟悉的线性回归例子。线性回归中的原始目标函数是标准的最小二乘经验风险：R(w) = 1/2 ||Xw - y||^2。现在，假设我们严格地将噪声直接添加到输入矩阵X中。输入X变成了一个损坏的矩阵X_tilde，在数学上定义为 X_tilde = X + Z。这个噪声矩阵Z到底是什么？既然我们原始的数据集矩阵X有N行（对应N个不同的样本）和M列（对应M个不同的特征），我们可以认为矩阵Z具有完全相同的维度。Z的每一行都是添加到第i个训练样本的特定噪声向量。我们要求这些噪声向量是独立同分布（IID）的，从精确以零为中心的正态分布中提取，其协方差矩阵为 δ * I，其中δ代表方差的某个小正标量。从本质上讲，我们在所有特征中均匀地添加了各向同性高斯噪声。我们想要严格地分析这种注入如何改变优化目标以及最终学到的参数。我们修改后的、经过噪声正则化的目标变为 R_tilde(w) = 1/2 ||X_tilde w - y||^2。代入我们包含噪声的定义，它的计算结果为 1/2 ||(X + Z)w - y||^2。如果我们用代数方法展开这个平方范数，我们将剩下三个截然不同的项：原始的未损坏风险 1/2 ||Xw - y||^2、纯噪声惩罚项 1/2 ||Zw||^2，以及互相关项 (Zw)^T (Xw - y)。因为我们的噪声矩阵Z本质上是一个随机变量，我们必须评估这个修改后风险的期望值，记为 E[R_tilde(w)]。由于统计期望是一个线性算子，我们可以无缝地将其分别分配给每一项。互相关项的期望优雅地坍缩为零，因为噪声Z的均值正好为零。因此，我们唯一需要评估的就是中间项的期望：E[1/2 ||Zw||^2]。让我们简明扼要地写出来以发现规律。平方范数 ||Zw||^2 本质上只是所有N个样本中，第i个噪声行向量z_i与模型权重向量w之间点积平方的总和。所以我们得到 1/2 * sum( (z_i * w)^2 )。将期望算子严格拖入求和符号内部，我们仔细观察 E[(z_i * w)^2]。因为z_i是一个具有对角协方差 δ * I 的正态分布随机向量，这种平方投影的期望在数学上简单地计算为 δ * ||w||^2。由于我们在所有N个样本上相同地对这个相同的期望值求和，纯噪声项的总期望整洁地变成了 1/2 * N * δ * ||w||^2。因此，我们修改后目标的期望值恰好等于原始经验目标 R(w) 加上一个严格的惩罚项：1/2 * (N * δ) * ||w||^2。这个结构形状看起来熟悉吗？在期望上，动态地将高斯噪声注入到线性回归模型的输入中，在数学上等同于应用标准的解析L2正则化，其中等效的正则化强度α简单地等于N乘以δ。因此，看起来我们一遍又一遍地重复着完全相同的故事！在线性领域，无论是我们显式地使用L2惩罚、实现提前停止，还是随机注入输入噪声，它们都不可思议地在代数上坍缩为等效的L2权重衰减形式。当然，永远要明确记住，这种完美的数学等价性仅适用于纯线性模型；高度非线性的神经网络在数学上无法保证表现完全一致，尽管它们宏观的正则化效果仍然高度相似。  

  - [16:22 - 16:28] **Label Smoothing, Weight Noise, and Summary**

    That covers adding noise to the input. You could alternatively choose to explicitly add noise directly to the outputs or labels. The reason you might do this heavily depends on your specific operational application. For example, in a standard classification problem, you typically use one-hot encoding for your categorical targets. A purely one-hot vector implies you are absolutely 100% confident about the ground-truth labels—assigning probability 1 for the correct class and exactly 0 for all other classes. However, if you strongly believe your dataset might contain human labeling errors, or if the decision boundary is intrinsically ambiguous and not clean-cut, maintaining such absolute confidence is mathematically inappropriate. In that precise case, you can explicitly add noise to the outputs, a technique broadly known in the industry as "label smoothing". Instead of stubbornly targeting a hard 1, you pragmatically reduce the target probability of the correct class down to 1 - α, and you distribute the remaining probability mass α evenly across the other K-1 incorrect classes, giving them a softened target of α / (K - 1). Another valid scenario for output noise is when you are pre-training a model on artificially generated or simulated data that unfortunately lacks the natural, messy noise of the real world; injecting artificial noise directly into the simulated outputs helps the model robustly transition to real-world deployment. Lastly, let's briefly touch upon the concept of adding noise to the model weights themselves during the active training loop. Suppose you have a deep neural network computing a standard prediction y_hat = f(x, θ). In each training step, we deliberately add a small random perturbation to the parameters, meaning θ explicitly becomes θ + δφ, where φ is drawn from a standard normal distribution N(0, I). If we mathematically perform a Taylor expansion to evaluate the expected empirical risk under this continuous perturbation, we discover that the expected risk equals the original risk plus a fascinating additional penalty term: (m * δ^2 / 2) * ||∇_θ f(x, θ)||^2. Please closely notice that this is fundamentally different from L1 or standard L2 regularization. We are notably not penalizing the absolute magnitude of the weights themselves; rather, we are strictly penalizing the L2 norm of the gradient of the neural network's predictions with respect to the weights. In other words, this mathematically penalizes extreme, chaotic variations in the network's output caused by tiny microscopic changes in the weights, actively forcing the network to settle into wide, flat, computationally stable regions of the loss landscape rather than sharp, fragile local minima. Executing this results in a finalized model that is significantly less sensitive to parameter quantization or small hardware memory fluctuations. Let's systematically summarize the overarching regularization strategies we've discussed today. First, we extensively explored Parameter Norm Penalties, primarily L1 and L2, which actively modify the analytical loss function to constrain weight sizes. Second, we examined Early Stopping, which acts as a dynamic meta-algorithm modifying the core training procedure itself to intelligently halt before catastrophic overfitting occurs. Finally, we intensely looked at Injecting Noise into the inputs, outputs, or weights, which can be thought of as artificially modifying your raw data or network state to forcefully embed robustness priors. For all these distinct methods, we relied heavily on simple linear models to analyze their exact mathematical effects. The recurring, elegant theoretical theme is that, under strict linear assumptions, both early stopping and input noise injection smoothly map mathematically to standard L2 weight decay. However, because we deploy highly nonlinear deep neural networks in reality, these techniques naturally provide completely distinct structural benefits in practice. Consequently, in cutting-edge practice, we typically don't just lazily pick one; we robustly combine several of these techniques simultaneously to achieve absolute optimal generalization across our models.  

    以上涵盖了向输入添加噪声的内容。或者，你也可以选择直接向输出或标签显式地添加噪声。你这样做的原因在很大程度上取决于你的具体操作应用。例如，在标准的分类问题中，你通常对你的类别目标使用独热（one-hot）编码。一个纯粹的独热向量意味着你对真实的标签绝对有100%的信心——为正确的类别分配概率1，为所有其他类别精确分配0。然而，如果你强烈认为你的数据集可能包含人为的标记错误，或者如果决策边界本质上是模糊的而不是一刀切的，保持这种绝对的信心在数学上是不合适的。在那种特定情况下，你可以显式地向输出添加噪声，这项技术在业界被广泛称为“标签平滑（label smoothing）”。你不再固执地瞄准一个硬性的1，而是务实地将正确类别的目标概率降低到 1 - α，并将剩余的概率质量α均匀地分布在其他 K-1 个不正确的类别中，给它们一个 α / (K - 1) 的软化目标。输出噪声的另一个有效场景是，当你在人工生成或模拟的数据上预训练模型，而这些数据不幸缺乏现实世界自然、杂乱的噪声时；直接将人为噪声注入到模拟输出中，有助于模型稳健地过渡到现实世界的部署。最后，让我们简要谈谈在主动训练循环中向模型权重本身添加噪声的概念。假设你有一个深度神经网络在计算标准预测 y_hat = f(x, θ)。在每个训练步骤中，我们故意对参数添加一个小的随机扰动，这意味着θ显式地变成了 θ + δφ，其中φ是从标准正态分布 N(0, I) 中提取的。如果我们在数学上执行泰勒展开，以评估这种连续扰动下的预期经验风险，我们发现预期风险等于原始风险加上一个引人入胜的额外惩罚项：(m * δ^2 / 2) * ||∇_θ f(x, θ)||^2。请密切注意，这与L1或标准的L2正则化有着根本的不同。我们并没有去惩罚权重本身的绝对量级；相反，我们严格惩罚的是神经网络预测相对于权重的梯度的L2范数。换句话说，这在数学上惩罚了由权重的微小变化引起的网络输出的极端、混乱的变化，主动迫使网络稳定在损失地形中宽阔、平坦、计算稳定的区域，而不是尖锐、脆弱的局部极小值。执行此操作将产生一个最终模型，该模型对参数量化或微小的硬件内存波动明显不那么敏感。让我们系统地总结一下今天讨论的总体正则化策略。首先，我们广泛探讨了参数范数惩罚，主要是L1和L2，它们主动修改解析损失函数以约束权重大小。其次，我们考察了提前停止，它作为一种动态的元算法，修改核心训练过程本身，以便在发生灾难性的过拟合之前智能地停止。最后，我们深入研究了向输入、输出或权重注入噪声，这可以被认为是人为地修改原始数据或网络状态，以强制嵌入稳健性先验。对于所有这些不同的方法，我们严重依赖简单的线性模型来分析它们确切的数学效果。反复出现的、优雅的理论主题是，在严格的线性假设下，提前停止和输入噪声注入都在数学上平滑地映射到标准的L2权重衰减。然而，因为我们在现实中部署的是高度非线性的深度神经网络，这些技术在实践中自然提供了完全不同的结构性好处。因此，在最前沿的实践中，我们通常不会只是偷懒地选择一个；我们稳健地同时结合其中几种技术，以在我们的模型中实现绝对最佳的泛化。  

  - [16:28 - 16:34] **Coding Demo: Early Stopping and Gaussian Noise in Keras**

    If there are no lingering theoretical questions, let's jump straight into the Python code demo to wrap up for the day. I will practically show you how straightforward it is to computationally implement both early stopping and noise injection inside the Keras framework. Let me explicitly zoom in on the screen so you can read the syntax. We are returning to the custom `train_and_save` wrapper function we built earlier today. The standard `model.fit()` method in Keras accepts an incredibly powerful parameter called `callbacks`. This is the exact software mechanism we use to implement early stopping. We natively import `EarlyStopping` directly from `keras.callbacks`. When we instantiate this callback object, we can aggressively configure several critical behavioral parameters. First is precisely what metric we are actually monitoring during training; the standard default is monitoring `val_loss`. Next is `min_delta`, which analytically defines the absolute minimum change in the monitored metric that mathematically qualifies as an actual valid improvement. The default is 0, meaning any microscopic fractional decrease immediately resets the patience threshold. Finally, you set the pivotal `patience` parameter, which strictly dictates exactly how many consecutive training epochs you are willing to patiently tolerate with absolutely no valid improvement before you abruptly terminate the entire training loop. The optimal empirical values for `patience` and `min_delta` actually depend somewhat heavily on your chosen batch size. If you are computing gradients using very small batch sizes, your gradient updates are intrinsically more volatile and noisy, so your validation loss curve will naturally bounce around erratically. In that specific scenario, you might want to purposefully configure a strictly positive `min_delta` and a slightly larger `patience` value to prevent the algorithm from prematurely terminating due to standard stochastic training noise. We simply pass this configured `EarlyStopping` object directly into the `callbacks` list parameter within our `model.fit()` call. When we fully execute the training block and plot the resulting learning curve, we can visually see the algorithm actively in action. The plotted validation loss drops smoothly, hits a numerical minimum, and begins to subtly curve back upwards. The active callback dynamically detects this, patiently waits for a sequence of 5 consecutive non-improving validation steps, and then cleanly halts the execution right there, successfully preventing the severe, diverging overfitting we clearly witnessed in our initial unregularized baseline model. Now, how do we computationally implement data noise injection? It is equally simple. Keras automatically provides a dedicated, purpose-built layer cleanly called `GaussianNoise`. In our sequential model architecture definition, we physically insert this `GaussianNoise` layer immediately after our initial input layer, passing in our specifically desired variance parameter. But we absolutely don't have to stop just there; we can also inject this explicit noise directly into the internal hidden feature representations by inserting additional `GaussianNoise` layers immediately after the dense hidden nodes at every single layer of the deep network. The exceptionally beautiful thing about relying on the Keras `GaussianNoise` layer is that it only actively injects noise during the active training phase; it automatically completely disables itself during inference and validation evaluation, perfectly matching our theoretical framework without requiring manual boolean toggles. When we run this heavily noise-injected model, you will immediately visually notice that the training accuracy no longer artificially rockets straight to a flawless 100%. The model is constantly fighting the chaotic injected noise, actively preventing it from flawlessly memorizing the training dataset, which fundamentally acts as a massively powerful regularizer. Implementing these advanced techniques is highly straightforward. On their own, a single isolated technique might not perfectly stabilize a highly complex, high-dimensional dataset, so I highly encourage you to computationally experiment with combining L2, early stopping, and network noise injection all together. Next week, we will formally introduce another incredibly popular structural regularization technique called Dropout, so I will firmly save that discussion for then.

    如果没有遗留的理论问题，让我们直接跳入Python代码演示来结束今天的内容。我将实际向你们展示在Keras框架内通过代码实现提前停止和噪声注入是多么的直接。让我明确地放大屏幕，以便你们能看清语法。我们回到今天早些时候构建的自定义`train_and_save`包装函数。Keras中标准的`model.fit()`方法接受一个极其强大的参数，名为`callbacks`（回调）。这正是我们用来实现提前停止的软件机制。我们直接从`keras.callbacks`原生导入`EarlyStopping`。当我们实例化这个回调对象时，我们可以积极地配置几个关键的行为参数。首先是我们实际上在训练期间精确监控什么指标；标准默认是监控`val_loss`（验证损失）。其次是`min_delta`，它解析性地定义了在数学上符合实际有效改进标准的监控指标的绝对最小变化量。默认值为0，这意味着任何微小的分数级下降都会立即重置耐心阈值。最后，您设置关键的`patience`（耐心）参数，它严格规定了在你突然终止整个训练循环之前，你愿意耐心容忍多少个连续的没有任何有效改进的训练周期。`patience`和`min_delta`的最佳经验值实际上在一定程度上严重依赖于你选择的批次大小（batch size）。如果你使用非常小的批次大小来计算梯度，你的梯度更新本质上会更具挥发性和噪声，因此你的验证损失曲线自然会不规律地上下波动。在那种特定情况下，你可能会有意配置一个严格为正的`min_delta`和稍大的`patience`值，以防止算法由于标准的随机训练噪声而过早终止。我们只需将这个配置好的`EarlyStopping`对象直接传递到`model.fit()`调用中的`callbacks`列表参数中即可。当我们完全执行训练代码块并绘制生成的学习曲线时，我们可以直观地看到该算法在积极发挥作用。绘制的验证损失平滑下降，达到数值上的最小值，然后开始微妙地向上弯曲回升。激活的回调动态地检测到这一点，耐心地等待连续5个没有改进的验证步骤的序列，然后干净利落地在那里停止执行，成功地防止了我们在最初未正则化的基线模型中清楚地看到的严重的、发散的过拟合。现在，我们如何在代码上实现数据噪声注入？同样简单。Keras自动提供了一个专用的、专门构建的层，干净利落地称为`GaussianNoise`。在我们的顺序模型架构定义中，我们在初始输入层之后立即物理插入这个`GaussianNoise`层，传入我们特别期望的方差参数。但我们绝对不必就此止步；我们也可以通过在深度网络每一层的密集隐藏节点之后立即插入额外的`GaussianNoise`层，将这种显式噪声直接注入到内部隐藏特征表示中。依赖Keras的`GaussianNoise`层极其美妙的一点是，它只在活跃的训练阶段主动注入噪声；它在推理和验证评估期间完全自动禁用自身，完美地符合我们的理论框架，而无需手动切换布尔值。当我们运行这个重度注入噪声的模型时，你会在视觉上立刻注意到训练准确率不再人为地径直飙升到完美的100%。模型不断地与混乱的注入噪声作斗争，积极防止它完美地记住训练数据集，这从根本上起到了极其强大的正则化器的作用。实现这些高级技术非常直接。单独来看，一种孤立的技术可能无法完美地稳定高度复杂、高维度的数据集，所以我强烈鼓励大家在计算上进行实验，将L2、提前停止和网络噪声注入结合在一起。下周，我们将正式介绍另一种极其流行的结构正则化技术，称为Dropout，所以我将坚决地把那部分讨论留到那时。

## Week 7
  - [14:00:00 - 14:10:00] **Course Updates and Introduction to Model Ensembling**

    Welcome back, everyone, to Lecture 7 of Deep Learning and Applications. Before we dive into today's material, I want to briefly mention that the project proposals have been graded. Please make sure you are working actively together and contributing to your group. Remember that group member contributions will be peer-reviewed at the end of the semester, and individual marks will be subject to moderation based on those reviews, so please do your fair share. Last time, we introduced some methods to improve model performance, specifically parameter norm penalties, early stopping, and injecting noise. Those methods mostly leave the core model architecture intact. Today, we are going to look at some other popular techniques, some of which actually involve changing the model's architecture. We will start with model ensembling, specifically a technique called Bootstrap Aggregating, or Bagging. Bagging is the simplest method for combining models. The idea is that we train m different models, let's call them f_1 through f_m, on random subsamples of our training data. Then, we combine them in an obvious way to make predictions. For regression, we just take the average of all the models' predictions. For classification, we take the mode, or the majority vote, though you could alternatively use other voting schemes. To visualize this, imagine a dataset with three points. We might create three new training sets by sampling with replacement. The first model might be trained on point 1 and 2, the second on 2 and 3, and the third on 1 twice. Then we aggregate them. To understand mathematically why this helps, let's consider a simple scalar model where the prediction of the i-th model, f_i(x), is equal to the true oracle function, f*(x), plus some noise term, epsilon_i(x).

    欢迎大家回到深度学习与应用第七讲。在深入探讨今天的内容之前，我想简要提一下，项目提案已经批改完毕。请确保你们在小组中积极合作并做出贡献。请记住，在学期末将进行小组成员贡献的同行评审，个人分数将根据这些评审进行调整，所以请务必尽到自己的责任。上次课，我们介绍了一些提高模型性能的方法，特别是参数范数惩罚、提前停止和注入噪声。这些方法主要是保持核心模型架构完整不变的。今天，我们将研究其他一些流行的技术，其中一些实际上涉及到改变模型的架构。我们将从模型集成开始，具体来说是一种称为自举汇聚法，或Bagging的技术。Bagging 是组合模型最简单的方法。我们的想法是，我们在训练数据的随机子样本上训练 m 个不同的模型，我们把它们叫做 f_1 到 f_m。然后，我们用一种显而易见的方式将它们结合起来进行预测。对于回归任务，我们只需取所有模型预测的平均值。对于分类任务，我们取众数或多数投票，尽管您也可以使用其他的投票方案。为了形象地说明这一点，想象一个包含三个数据点的数据集。我们可以通过有放回抽样创建三个新的训练集。第一个模型可能在点1和点2上训练，第二个在点2和点3上训练，第三个在点1上训练两次。然后我们将它们汇总。为了从数学上理解为什么这会有帮助，让我们考虑一个简单的标量模型，其中第 i 个模型的预测 f_i(x) 等于真实的预言机函数 f*(x) 加上一些噪声项 epsilon_i(x)。

  - [14:10:00 - 14:14:07] **The Mathematical Motivation for Bagging**

    Each model comes with some noise term epsilon_i. On average, the expected value of this noise is zero, but there is some non-zero variance. Importantly, we make an assumption that the noise terms from each model are uncorrelated. Meaning that the expected value of the product of any two distinct noise terms, epsilon_i and epsilon_j, is zero. If they are the same term, then the expected value of the square is just the variance, which is some sigma squared. So, what we want to do is compare the errors. Let E(x) be the average of the expected errors from each of your individual models f_i. Each of your models f_i has some error epsilon_i, so E(x) is just the average error of each model. Since each model gives you a variance of sigma squared, and there are m of them, the average is just sigma squared. It's just the variance of one noise term. Now, let E bar of x be the expected error of the aggregate model, f bar. The aggregate model is the average of the individual f_i models. So we are asking, what is the expected error of the average of f_i minus the true function f*? I can put the f* inside the sum by imagining I have m copies of it, and then multiplying by one over m outside. That means f_i(x) minus f*(x) leaves us with just the noise term, epsilon_i(x). So we need to square the sum of epsilon_i(x) multiplied by one over m. Expanding this gives you a double sum, with a one over m squared factor in front. In this double sum, because we assumed the noise terms are uncorrelated, only the terms where i equals j survive. If i is not equal to j, we get zero contribution. For the terms where i equals j, you just have the expected value of epsilon_i squared, which is the variance, sigma squared. So you have one over m squared times the sum from one to m of sigma squared. This simplifies to one over m times sigma squared, which is exactly one over m times the average error of each individual term. This tells you that the aggregate model will have a factor of one over m of the error of each term individually. This demonstrates that if we aggregate the models, if we do bagging, you can get a significantly lower error. That is the primary mathematical motivation for bagging.

    每个模型都带有一些噪声项 epsilon_i。平均而言，这个噪声的期望值为零，但存在一些非零的方差。重要的是，我们假设来自每个模型的噪声项是不相关的。这意味着任意两个不同噪声项 epsilon_i 和 epsilon_j 乘积的期望值为零。如果它们是相同的项，那么平方的期望值就是方差，也就是某个 sigma 的平方。那么，我们要做的是比较这些误差。令 E(x) 为每个单独模型 f_i 的预期误差的平均值。你的每个模型 f_i 都有一些误差 epsilon_i，所以 E(x) 只是每个模型的平均误差。因为每个模型给你的方差是 sigma 平方，并且有 m 个这样的模型，所以平均值就是 sigma 平方。它只是一个噪声项的方差。现在，令 E bar (x) 为聚合模型 f bar 的预期误差。聚合模型是个体 f_i 模型的平均值。所以我们在问，f_i 的平均值减去真实函数 f* 的预期误差是多少？我可以通过想象我有 m 个 f* 的副本，把它放到求和符号里面，然后在外面乘以 m 分之一。这意味着 f_i(x) 减去 f*(x) 只剩下噪声项 epsilon_i(x)。所以我们需要将 epsilon_i(x) 的总和乘以 m 分之一后进行平方。展开这个式子会得到一个双重求和，前面有一个 m 平方分之一的因子。在这个双重求和中，因为我们假设噪声项是不相关的，所以只有 i 等于 j 的项才会保留下来。如果 i 不等于 j，贡献就为零。对于 i 等于 j 的项，你只有 epsilon_i 平方的期望值，也就是方差 sigma 平方。所以你有 m 平方分之一乘以从 1 到 m 的 sigma 平方的总和。这简化为 m 分之一乘以 sigma 平方，这正好是个体项平均误差的 m 分之一。这告诉你，聚合模型的误差是个体项误差的 m 分之一。这表明，如果我们聚合这些模型，如果我们使用 Bagging，你可以得到显著降低的误差。这就是 Bagging 的主要数学动机。

  - [14:14:07 - 14:17:21] **The Bias-Variance Trade-off in Bagging**

    So the question is, it seems like there is only an advantage to bagging and no disadvantage. We analyzed this and concluded that if you aggregate, you get an advantage of reduced variance, a reduction in the error term. Does that mean you should always aggregate? We need to look closely at the assumptions we made to end up with this conclusion, and ask if those assumptions are realistic. The key assumptions we made were that we have zero bias, and that each of the models are completely uncorrelated. Are these assumptions realistic in practice? If we think about the way we do the subsampling, if your subsample size is small, then each individual model will have a high bias. In order to satisfy the first assumption of zero bias, your subsample size must be large. However, if the subsample size is large, that means your different subsamples will inevitably overlap heavily. If the data overlaps heavily, that means the models f_i trained on them are not uncorrelated anymore. So you basically find that it's impossible to satisfy both assumptions perfectly at the same time. The assumptions are strictly not realistic. If you have large subsamples, the models become highly correlated. If you have small subsamples, they are not correlated, but each of them will have significant non-zero biases. This is a classic example of the bias-variance trade-off. However, if you choose reasonable sizes for the subsamples, you can usually strike a balance and still get a performance improvement most of the time. Just keep that limitation in mind. Despite the assumptions not being fully realistic, we are often somewhere in between those extremes, and you can still gain a big advantage. Therefore, bagging is still an extremely useful technique.

    那么问题来了，似乎 Bagging 只有优点没有缺点。我们分析了这一点并得出结论，如果你进行聚合，你会得到减少方差的好处，即误差项的减少。这是否意味着你应该总是进行聚合？我们需要仔细研究导致这个结论的假设，并问问这些假设是否现实。我们做出的关键假设是我们有零偏差，并且每个模型都是完全不相关的。这些假设在实践中现实吗？如果我们考虑进行子采样的方式，如果你的子样本量很小，那么每个单独的模型都会有很高的偏差。为了满足零偏差的第一个假设，你的子样本量必须很大。然而，如果子样本量很大，这意味着你不同的子样本将不可避免地发生严重重叠。如果数据严重重叠，这意味着在它们上面训练的模型 f_i 就不再是不相关的了。所以你基本上会发现，不可能同时完美地满足这两个假设。这些假设严格来说是不现实的。如果你有大的子样本，模型就会变得高度相关。如果你有小的子样本，它们是不相关的，但每个模型都会有显著的非零偏差。这是偏差-方差权衡的经典例子。然而，如果你为子样本选择合理的尺寸，你通常可以取得平衡，并且在大多数情况下仍然获得性能提升。只需牢记这个限制。尽管假设并非完全现实，但我们通常处于这些极端情况之间的某个位置，你仍然可以获得很大的优势。因此，Bagging 仍然是一种极其有用的技术。

  - [14:17:21 - 14:21:34] **Introduction to Dropout as Feature Bagging**

    The major disadvantage of bagging or any model ensembling technique is that you have to train and store multiple models. Instead of just one model, you now have m models. Your deep learning models are quite big, so it would be computationally very expensive and memory-intensive to actually train and store multiple deep neural networks. To address this, we are going to talk about a technique called dropout. The best way to think about the motivation of dropout is that it is an efficient way to approximately construct model ensembles. The idea is similar to doing stochastic gradient descent instead of full-batch gradient descent. Instead of explicitly training m models, I am going to randomly sample a model each time and take a prediction from that. That is really the key idea. Instead of computing the sum of m terms, each time I am just going to get a contribution from a random sub-network, which is drawn from one of the massive ensemble of models implicitly defined by the network. The type of bagging we talked about in the last few slides is called sample bagging, where we take random subsamples of the training data. But there is another popular technique called feature bagging, which means I take random subsamples of the features of my data to train the model. Dropout, as we will describe it, is actually trying to approximate feature bagging. For a neural network, dropout implements averaging by applying a random mask over the nodes or weights. Let's think of it in terms of the connections: during training, I will randomly drop out certain weights or nodes of the neural network. That temporarily gives me a much simpler model. By throwing away some of the features or learned connections at random, it acts similarly to feature bagging. Normally, we are dealing with very big networks. In a very small example network, dropping out edges at random might completely disconnect the input from the output. But in the large networks we use in practice, this total disconnection issue is extremely unlikely to happen; you will almost always retain a connected path from input to output. Because you are randomly dropping out edges, you are implicitly generating a massive number of these simpler sub-models, making it very similar to an ensemble built via feature bagging.

    Bagging 或任何模型集成技术的主要缺点是，你必须训练和存储多个模型。现在你不再只有一个模型，而是有 m 个模型。你的深度学习模型非常庞大，因此实际训练和存储多个深度神经网络在计算上将非常昂贵且消耗内存。为了解决这个问题，我们将讨论一种称为 Dropout 的技术。理解 Dropout 动机的最好方法是，把它看作是一种近似构建模型集成的有效方法。这个想法类似于使用随机梯度下降而不是全批量梯度下降。我不需要明确地训练 m 个模型，我只需要每次随机抽样一个模型并从中获取预测。这才是真正的核心理念。我不再计算 m 个项的总和，而是每次只从一个随机的子网络中获取贡献，这个子网络是从网络隐式定义的庞大模型集合中提取出来的。我们在前几张幻灯片中讨论的那种 Bagging 称为样本 Bagging，即我们抽取训练数据的随机子样本。但还有另一种流行的技术称为特征 Bagging，这意味着我抽取数据的特征的随机子样本来训练模型。我们即将描述的 Dropout 实际上试图近似特征 Bagging。对于神经网络，Dropout 通过在节点或权重上应用随机掩码来实现平均。让我们从连接的角度来考虑：在训练期间，我将随机丢弃神经网络的某些权重或节点。这会暂时给我一个简单得多的模型。通过随机丢弃一些特征或学到的连接，它的作用类似于特征 Bagging。通常，我们处理的是非常大的网络。在一个非常小的示例网络中，随机丢弃边缘可能会完全切断输入与输出之间的连接。但在我们实际使用的大型网络中，这种完全断开的问题极不可能发生；你几乎总是能保留一条从输入到输出的连通路径。因为你随机丢弃了边缘，你隐含地生成了大量这些更简单的子模型，使其非常类似于通过特征 Bagging 构建的集成。

  - [14:21:34 - 14:26:09] **Mathematical Formulation and Weight Scaling Inference Rule**

    Mathematically, we can write a neural network layer with dropout similarly to a standard fully connected layer. The only difference is that we introduce a new term, a vector mask which we will denote as mu. A mask means it is a vector with values of either exactly zero or one for each coordinate. We apply this using a Hadamard product, denoted by a circle, which means element-wise multiplication. It does not change the dimension. We take our original input feature vector x, and we multiply some elements by zero, dropping them out, while the others we multiply by one, leaving them unchanged. When doing stochastic gradient descent, for each step of training, we randomly sample a new mask mu. Each iteration uses a different mask, meaning it drops out different weights or different nodes. The new mask mu is drawn identically and independently distributed, with each coordinate being one with a probability p, and zero with a probability 1 - p. This probability p is called the keep probability, while 1 - p is the dropout rate. Note that if we implement this, the expected value of the vector mask mu is simply a vector of p's. This means that, on average during training, you are effectively shrinking the overall activations and features by a factor of p. This behavior happens during training. However, during inference or prediction, we want to use the full capacity of the model, so we do not perform dropout. We set the full mask to be all ones, meaning no nodes are dropped. But here's the catch: because your weights were implicitly trained under a regime where the signal was reduced by a factor of p, applying all ones during inference would make the activations too large. To compensate, during inference, we must multiply each weight by a factor of p. This ensures everything remains on the same scale during both training and prediction. This step is known as the weight scaling inference rule, and it is automatically handled by frameworks like TensorFlow. Are there any questions so far? Okay, to recap, dropout is trying to mimic a massive model ensemble where each different mask represents a distinct model. If we were truly doing model ensembling, a network with m nodes could theoretically form 2^m different models, which would be impossibly costly. Dropout gives us an efficient way to randomly sample from this massive ensemble every training iteration.

    在数学上，我们可以将带有 Dropout 的神经网络层写成类似于标准全连接层的形式。唯一的区别是，我们引入了一个新项，一个我们将记为 mu 的向量掩码。掩码意味着它是一个向量，其每个坐标的值要么恰好是 0，要么是 1。我们使用哈达玛积（用圆圈表示）来应用它，这意味着逐元素相乘。它不会改变维度。我们提取原始输入特征向量 x，并将某些元素乘以 0（即将它们丢弃），而其他元素则乘以 1（保持不变）。在进行随机梯度下降时，对于训练的每一步，我们随机抽样一个新的掩码 mu。每次迭代都使用不同的掩码，这意味着它丢弃不同的权重或不同的节点。新掩码 mu 是独立同分布抽取的，每个坐标为 1 的概率为 p，为 0 的概率为 1 - p。这个概率 p 称为保留概率，而 1 - p 则是 Dropout 率。请注意，如果我们实现这一点，向量掩码 mu 的期望值就是一个全是 p 的向量。这意味着，在训练期间平均而言，你实际上是将整体激活和特征缩小了 p 的因子。这种行为发生在训练期间。然而，在推理或预测期间，我们希望利用模型的全部容量，所以我们不进行 Dropout。我们将完整掩码设置为全 1，这意味着没有节点被丢弃。但问题来了：因为你的权重是在信号减少了 p 倍的机制下隐式训练的，如果在推理过程中应用全 1，将会使激活变得过大。为了补偿，在推理过程中，我们必须将每个权重乘以因子 p。这确保了在训练和预测期间所有东西都保持在相同的尺度上。这一步被称为权重缩放推理规则，它由 TensorFlow 等框架自动处理。到目前为止有任何问题吗？好的，总结一下，Dropout 试图模仿一个庞大的模型集成，其中每个不同的掩码代表一个独特的模型。如果我们真的在进行模型集成，一个具有 m 个节点的网络理论上可以形成 2^m 个不同的模型，这将耗费极其高昂的成本。Dropout 为我们提供了一种有效的方法，可以在每次训练迭代时从这个庞大的集成中随机抽样。

If we cleverly add and subtract the term XW, which represents the standard prediction without dropout, we can split the loss into parts. Expanding this gives us the normal linear regression loss term, plus a cross term, plus a completely new third term. This is because the expectation of D_mu is simply p times the identity matrix, so dividing by p gives the identity, making the term inside the expectation zero.   - [14:40:00 - 15:01:00] **Motivation for Batch Normalization**

    Let's consider a simple, one-dimensional, deep linear neural network to understand the motivation behind batch normalization. Imagine a network where the prediction $\hat{y}$ is just the input $x$ multiplied by a sequence of weights $w_1, w_2$, all the way up to $w_l$. We're assuming no non-linearities for this simplified example to build intuition. Let's suppose we are using the squared error loss function, and for a specific data point, the correct label $y$ is exactly zero. The loss $L(w)$ is then simply one half of $\hat{y}$ squared. If we make a standard gradient descent update, the new weight vector $w'$ will be the old weight vector $w$ minus the learning rate $\epsilon$ times the gradient $g$. We can analyze the new loss $L(w')$ using a Taylor expansion. The new loss is approximately the old loss $L(w)$ minus $\epsilon$ times the squared norm of the gradient $g$, plus terms of order $\epsilon^2$. Gradient descent works and decreases the loss function *only if* the learning rate $\epsilon$ is sufficiently small, such that the linear term (which decreases the loss) dominates the higher-order $\epsilon^2$ terms (which might increase it). However, in a deep network, expanding the prediction after the weight update reveals a problem. The new prediction is the product of all the updated weights. When you expand this product, you get the original prediction, minus a sum of terms linear in $\epsilon$, plus a sum of cross-terms of order $\epsilon^2$, and so on. If we plug this expanded prediction back into our squared loss function and look specifically at the order $\epsilon^2$ terms, we find expressions that involve the product of many un-updated weights. For example, a term might look like $\epsilon^2 \cdot x \cdot \hat{y} \cdot g_1 \cdot g_2 \cdot (w_3 \cdot w_4 \cdots w_l)$. For gradient descent to consistently decrease the loss, this $\epsilon^2$ term must remain relatively small. However, this term contains the product of many weights ($w_3 \cdots w_l$). If the scale of these weights is even slightly greater than 1, their product can easily become extremely large, making the $\epsilon^2$ term dominate and ruining the descent property unless $\epsilon$ is made impractically tiny. This illustrates a fundamental problem in deep networks: the optimal learning rate choice depends heavily on the scales and magnitudes of weights and activations at different layers. Because these scales constantly change as training proceeds, it becomes very difficult to choose a single, constant learning rate that works well throughout the entire training process. This is the primary motivation for batch normalization: if we can normalize the values at each layer so that their scales and statistics remain similar and bounded throughout training, we can decouple the layers and use larger, more stable learning rates.

    为了理解批量归一化 (Batch Normalization) 背后的动机，让我们考虑一个简单的一维深度线性神经网络。想象一个网络，其预测 $\hat{y}$ 只是输入 $x$ 乘以一系列权重 $w_1, w_2$，一直到 $w_l$。为了建立直觉，在这个简化的例子中我们假设没有非线性。假设我们使用平方误差损失函数，并且对于一个特定的数据点，正确的标签 $y$ 正好是零。那么损失 $L(w)$ 仅仅是 $\hat{y}$ 平方的一半。如果我们进行标准的梯度下降更新，新的权重向量 $w'$ 将是旧权重向量 $w$ 减去学习率 $\epsilon$ 乘以梯度 $g$。我们可以使用泰勒展开式来分析新的损失 $L(w')$。新的损失大约等于旧损失 $L(w)$ 减去 $\epsilon$ 乘以梯度 $g$ 的平方范数，加上 $\epsilon^2$ 量级的项。梯度下降有效且能降低损失函数的前提是，**只有当**学习率 $\epsilon$ 足够小，使得线性项（降低损失）主导高阶 $\epsilon^2$ 项（可能增加损失）时。然而，在深度网络中，展开权重更新后的预测揭示了一个问题。新的预测是所有更新后权重的乘积。当你展开这个乘积时，你会得到原始预测，减去一个关于 $\epsilon$ 的线性项之和，加上一个 $\epsilon^2$ 量级的交叉项之和，依此类推。如果我们将这个展开的预测代回我们的平方损失函数中，并专门查看 $\epsilon^2$ 量级的项，我们会发现包含许多未更新权重乘积的表达式。例如，一个项可能看起来像 $\epsilon^2 \cdot x \cdot \hat{y} \cdot g_1 \cdot g_2 \cdot (w_3 \cdot w_4 \cdots w_l)$。为了使梯度下降持续降低损失，这个 $\epsilon^2$ 项必须保持相对较小。然而，这一项包含了许多权重的乘积 ($w_3 \cdots w_l$)。如果这些权重的尺度哪怕略大于1，它们的乘积也很容易变得极其巨大，使得 $\epsilon^2$ 项占据主导地位并破坏下降特性，除非将 $\epsilon$ 设置得不切实际地微小。这说明了深度网络中的一个根本问题：最佳学习率的选择在很大程度上取决于不同层中权重和激活的尺度和幅度。因为这些尺度在训练过程中不断变化，所以很难选择一个在整个训练过程中都能良好运作的单一恒定学习率。这就是批量归一化的主要动机：如果我们可以对每一层的值进行归一化，使得它们的尺度和统计数据在整个训练过程中保持相似且有界，我们就可以解耦各个层，并使用更大、更稳定的学习率。

  - [15:01:00 - 15:07:30] **The Problem with Deep Networks and Uncontrolled Scales**

    As we saw in the simplified linear example, if you have a deep network, the output is essentially the input $x$ multiplied by a long chain of weights, $w_1$ through $w_l$. If we use a squared loss where the correct label is zero, the loss is just the squared prediction. When we perform a gradient descent update, the new loss depends not just on the linear gradient step, but also on second-order terms derived from the Taylor expansion. We generally assume gradient descent works because these second-order terms, which are proportional to the learning rate epsilon squared, are very small. This requires epsilon to be small enough that the linear reduction term dominates. However, when we explicitly expanded the updated prediction in our deep linear model, we saw that the epsilon squared terms involve a sum over pairs of gradients multiplied by a long product of the remaining un-updated weights. Specifically, you get terms involving the product of $l-2$ weights. To ensure gradient descent is stable and actually decreases the loss, this term involving the long product of weights must remain small. The problem is that it is incredibly difficult to guarantee that the product of many weights stays small; if the weights are on average slightly larger than 1, their product will explode exponentially with depth. The critical lesson here is that for gradient descent to work effectively in deep neural networks, we need strict control over the scales or magnitudes of the weights and the resulting activations at every layer. Because these scales naturally drift and change as training proceeds, picking a single constant learning rate is often futile. If we want a stable training process, we need a mechanism to normalize these values at each layer, ensuring they maintain similar, well-behaved statistics throughout the entire training run. This concept of controlling the internal scales is the precise motivation that led to the development of Batch Normalization.

    正如我们在简化的线性例子中看到的，如果你有一个深度网络，输出本质上是输入 $x$ 乘以长长的一串权重，从 $w_1$ 到 $w_l$。如果我们使用正确标签为零的平方损失，损失仅仅是预测值的平方。当我们执行梯度下降更新时，新的损失不仅取决于线性梯度步长，还取决于源自泰勒展开式的二阶项。我们通常认为梯度下降之所以有效，是因为这些与学习率 epsilon 的平方成正比的二阶项非常小。这要求 epsilon 足够小，使得线性减少项占主导地位。然而，当我们在深度线性模型中明确展开更新后的预测时，我们看到 epsilon 平方项涉及一对梯度的总和乘以剩余未更新权重的长乘积。具体来说，你会得到包含 $l-2$ 个权重乘积的项。为了确保梯度下降是稳定的并真正减少损失，这个包含权重长乘积的项必须保持较小。问题在于，极难保证许多权重的乘积保持较小；如果权重平均略大于 1，它们的乘积将随深度呈指数爆炸。这里的关键教训是，为了使梯度下降在深度神经网络中有效运作，我们需要对每一层的权重尺度或幅度和产生的激活进行严格控制。因为这些尺度在训练过程中会自然漂移和改变，所以选择单一的恒定学习率通常是徒劳的。如果我们想要一个稳定的训练过程，我们需要一种机制在每一层对这些值进行归一化，确保它们在整个训练运行中保持相似、表现良好的统计特性。控制内部尺度的这一概念正是导致批量归一化发展的确切动机。

  - [15:07:30 - 15:10:33] **Defining the Batch Normalization Layer**

    So, what exactly is Batch Normalization? Let's say we are looking at a specific hidden layer in our network, and we are processing a mini-batch of size capital B. Within this batch, we have B different samples, and each sample produces a different hidden state value for that specific node. Let's call these hidden states $h^{(1)}$ through $h^{(B)}$. The batch normalization layer first calculates two batch-specific statistics: the empirical mean, denoted as $\mu$, which is just the average of the $h^{(i)}$ values across the batch, and the empirical standard deviation, denoted as $\sigma$, calculated across the same batch. The first step of the layer is to standardize the hidden state values within that specific batch. We subtract the batch mean $\mu$ and divide by the batch standard deviation $\sigma$. This operation forces the intermediate outputs of that layer, for that batch, to have a mean of exactly zero and a standard deviation of exactly one. However, if we only standardized the data, we would severely restrict the expressive power of the network, forcing every layer's output to always look like a standard normal distribution. To fix this and reintroduce expressive capacity, Batch Normalization includes a second step: rescaling and recentering. We introduce two new sets of trainable parameters for the layer, typically called $\gamma$ (gamma) for scale and $\beta$ (beta) for shift. After standardizing the values, we multiply them by $\gamma$ and add $\beta$. The crucial distinction here is that while the mean $\mu$ and standard deviation $\sigma$ are specific to the current batch and computed on the fly, the scale $\gamma$ and shift $\beta$ are parameters that are learned over time via backpropagation and are common across all batches. By doing this, we are effectively forcing every batch of data passing through this layer to adopt a specific scale and mean, but crucially, that scale and mean are governed by the trainable parameters $\gamma$ and $\beta$, allowing the network to learn the optimal scaling for that layer independently of the complex interactions happening in the layers below it.

    那么，究竟什么是批量归一化呢？假设我们正在查看网络中的一个特定的隐藏层，并且我们正在处理大小为大写 B 的小批量。在这个批次中，我们有 B 个不同的样本，每个样本都会为该特定节点产生不同的隐藏状态值。我们把这些隐藏状态称为 $h^{(1)}$ 到 $h^{(B)}$。批量归一化层首先计算两个特定于批次的统计量：经验均值（记为 $\mu$），它只是该批次中 $h^{(i)}$ 值的平均值；以及经验标准差（记为 $\sigma$），也在同一个批次中计算。该层的第一步是对该特定批次内的隐藏状态值进行标准化。我们减去批次均值 $\mu$ 并除以批次标准差 $\sigma$。这个操作迫使该层、该批次的中间输出的均值正好为零，标准差正好为一。然而，如果我们只标准化数据，我们将严重限制网络的表达能力，迫使每一层的输出总是看起来像标准正态分布。为了解决这个问题并重新引入表达能力，批量归一化包括第二步：重新缩放和重新居中。我们为该层引入了两组新的可训练参数，通常称为表示尺度的 $\gamma$ (gamma) 和表示平移的 $\beta$ (beta)。在标准化这些值之后，我们将它们乘以 $\gamma$ 并加上 $\beta$。这里的关键区别在于，虽然均值 $\mu$ 和标准差 $\sigma$ 是特定于当前批次并即时计算的，但尺度 $\gamma$ 和平移 $\beta$ 是随着时间通过反向传播学习到的参数，并且对于所有批次都是通用的。通过这样做，我们实际上是迫使通过这一层的每一批数据采用特定的尺度和均值，但关键是，该尺度和均值由可训练参数 $\gamma$ 和 $\beta$ 控制，从而允许网络独立于底层发生的复杂交互，学习到该层的最佳缩放比例。

  - [15:10:33 - 15:14:32] **Computational Graph, Placement, and Inference in Batch Norm**

    From an architectural standpoint, the Batch Normalization layer is uniquely interesting because it is the first model component we've discussed that explicitly couples different samples together. Previously, in a standard dense or convolutional layer, the operations on sample 1 were entirely independent of sample 2. But here, the standardization step requires calculating the mean and standard deviation across the entire mini-batch, meaning the processed output for one image directly depends on what other images happen to be in that same batch. When integrating this into a deep neural network, the typical convention is to place the Batch Normalization layer immediately after the linear transformation—like the matrix multiplication or the convolution—but before the non-linear activation function, like the ReLU. We apply it to $XW + b$ before putting it through the ReLU. A small but neat detail here is that if you place Batch Norm right after a linear layer, the bias term $b$ from that linear layer becomes completely redundant. The Batch Norm step will simply calculate the mean, which includes that constant bias, and subtract it right out. Therefore, when using Batch Norm, you can omit the bias terms in the preceding linear layers, saving parameters, because the role of the bias is entirely taken over by the trainable $\beta$ parameter within the Batch Norm layer itself. Now, everything we've discussed applies to training, where we operate on mini-batches. But what happens during inference or testing? During testing, we often process a single data point at a time. It's impossible to calculate a meaningful batch mean or standard deviation from a single sample. To resolve this, during the training phase, the Batch Norm layer silently keeps a running, moving average of the batch means $\mu$ and batch standard deviations $\sigma$ it encounters. Then, when the model is switched to inference mode, it stops calculating batch statistics on the fly. Instead, it uses those pre-computed moving averages to perform the standardization step. This ensures that the model acts deterministically during evaluation, processing a test sample completely independently of whatever else might be passing through the network at the same time.
