    自编码器不需要纯粹用于降维；它们可以被巧妙地改造以实现完全不同的任务。一个绝佳的例子是将自编码器变成一个强大的去噪算法。我们如何让自编码器对数据进行去噪呢？我们不是简单地将原始的、干净的数据 X 输入网络并让它重构 X 以最小化标准损失，而是故意破坏输入。我们取原始数据 X，故意注入噪声以创建一个受干扰的、脏的数据版本，我们称之为 X_tilde。然后，我们将这个嘈杂的 X_tilde 输入到自编码器的输入层。然而，我们不希望网络重构噪声；我们通过严格对比网络的最终输出与原始的、未受破坏的干净数据 X 来计算误差损失。通过迫使神经网络最小化给定嘈杂输入时自编码器的输出与干净目标数据之间的差异，模型内在学到了如何成功地识别并剥离信号中的噪声。我们实际上会在稍后的实践课程中实现这个特定的算法。一旦你完成了这个训练过程，你的自编码器就可以作为一个出色的模型，它已经学会了有效地对传入数据进行去噪。

    关于这点有什么问题吗？我们已经研究了自编码器的这些主要变体，但我应该提到，在实践中还有一些其他非常有趣且有用的架构。例如，有一种叫做序列到序列自编码器的东西。在这种设定中，你处理的是时间序列数据的序列。你不是使用标准的前馈层，而是利用循环神经网络（RNN）作为编码器，将序列压缩成潜在状态，然后再将其输入到另一个作为解码器的 RNN 中以重构序列。对于时间数据来说，这是一个非常有趣的方法。

    好了，我想我们现在休息一下。我们将有 10 分钟的休息时间，直到过五分——也就是下午 3:05——当我们回来时，我们将看看如何在代码中实际实现这些自编码器的一些想法。在休息期间，我也会尝试弄清楚 Canvas 上的某些设置。哦，如果你是 SSG 资助的学生，请记住正式记录你的出勤。

  - [15:06:09 - 15:10:45] **Building a Fully Connected Autoencoder for Images**

    Let's start looking at our demo on autoencoders. It is meant to be kind of a fun exercise. The dataset we are going to look at today is a Pokemon dataset—literally just a bunch of images of different Pokemon. Obviously, in practice, you would use much more serious datasets, like the medical X-rays we mentioned earlier, but this is a good, lighthearted dataset to test our ideas on. Let's just briefly visualize what this dataset looks like so you have a sense of the problem. As you can see, these are standard color images.

    Now, an important methodological point: even though we are doing unsupervised learning, we are still going to rigorously split our dataset into a training set and a testing set. We will train our autoencoder exclusively on the training set, and then we will evaluate its reconstruction performance on the unseen test set. We do this for the exact same reason we do it in supervised learning: we want to ensure that our model is actually learning generalizable underlying features of the data, not just explicitly memorizing the exact pixel patterns of the training images.

    Our input data here is actually quite high-dimensional. The images are 120 pixels by 120 pixels, and because they are color images, they have 3 RGB channels. So, the total number of input features for a single image is 120 times 120 times 3, which is 43,200 dimensions. That is a substantial input size. To start, let's try to build the simplest possible model: a fully connected autoencoder, basically just a sequence of dense neural network layers.

    For the encoder part, because we are using standard dense layers, the first thing we must do is take that 3D image block (120x120x3) and flatten it out into a single, long 1D vector. Once flattened, we feed it through two consecutive dense layers. We intentionally design these layers to aggressively bottleneck the information, reducing that massive 43,200-dimensional input all the way down to a latent dimension of just 32. So, we are compressing the image into a vector of length 32. The decoder part is built in the exact same style, just in reverse. It consists of a series of dense layers that take that 32-dimensional latent vector and progressively expand it back up. The final dense layer brings the dimension back up to the original size of 120 times 120 times 3.

    Notice a crucial detail here: for the final output layer of the decoder, we use a sigmoid activation function. Why do we do this? Well, prior to training, we performed a standard normalization step on our input images to ensure that every raw pixel value was scaled to fall strictly between 0 and 1. To ensure our reconstructed images also adhere to this valid pixel range, we use a sigmoid activation, which naturally bounds its output between 0 and 1. Finally, after the dense layers have generated this long vector, we simply reshape it back into the 120 by 120 by 3 spatial format so it can be interpreted and visualized as an image.

    Even though this conceptually is a "simple" dense autoencoder, if you actually look at the parameter count, it is staggering. Because the input and output layers are so massive (43,200 nodes), connecting them fully to the inner dense layers results in a huge number of weights. It has almost five million parameters for the encoder, and another five million for the decoder, resulting in something like ten or eleven million parameters in total. It is a massive, computationally heavy network. There is nothing particularly special about the training setup; we just use standard regularization and the default Adam optimizer.

    Let's train it and look at the results. We will first evaluate the trained autoencoder on our test set. We plot the original test images on the top row, and directly below them, we plot the autoencoder's reconstructed versions. Honestly, looking at them, the results are really not that great. To the human eye, they look like very blurry, washed-out color blobs. I don't think we can confidently identify most of the Pokemon from the reconstructions. It is clear that we should be able to do much better than this.

    Now, you might wonder if this poor performance is due to a massive generalization gap—meaning, did the model just overfit the training data and fail on the test data? To check this, we evaluate the exact same model on images from the training set. When we look at the original training images versus their reconstructions, we see that it performs just as badly on the training set. The reconstructions are equally blurry and awful. Therefore, the problem is not a generalization gap; the problem is that this dense autoencoder architecture itself is fundamentally no good for this task. The core issue, which we discussed in earlier lectures, is that fully connected neural networks are simply not the right tool for processing spatial image data.

    让我们开始看看关于自编码器的演示。这算是一个有趣的练习。我们今天要看的数据集是一个 Pokemon 数据集——字面上看就是一堆不同 Pokemon 的图像。显然，在实践中，你会使用更严肃的数据集，比如我们之前提到的医疗 X 光片，但这是一个用来测试我们想法的很好的、轻松的数据集。让我们简要地可视化一下这个数据集的样子，好让你们对问题有个概念。如你们所见，这些是标准的彩色图像。

    现在，一个重要的方法论观点：即使我们正在进行无监督学习，我们仍然会严格地将我们的数据集分成训练集和测试集。我们将只在训练集上训练我们的自编码器，然后我们将在未见过的测试集上评估它的重构性能。我们这样做的原因与在监督学习中完全一样：我们想要确保我们的模型实际上学到了数据可泛化的潜在特征，而不仅仅是死记硬背训练图像的确切像素模式。

    我们这里的输入数据实际上是相当高维的。图像是 120 像素乘 120 像素，并且因为它们是彩色图像，它们有 3 个 RGB 通道。所以，单张图像的输入特征总数是 120 乘 120 乘 3，即 43,200 维。这是一个相当大的输入尺寸。首先，让我们尝试构建一个最简单的可能模型：一个全连接自编码器，基本上就是一连串的密集神经网络层。

    对于编码器部分，因为我们使用的是标准的密集层，我们必须做的第一件事就是把那个 3D 图像块（120x120x3）展平为一个单一的、长的一维向量。一旦展平，我们就把它送入两个连续的密集层。我们有意设计这些层来积极地限制信息流通（制造瓶颈），将那个庞大的 43,200 维输入一直降低到只有 32 维的潜在维度。所以，我们正在将图像压缩成一个长度为 32 的向量。解码器部分以完全相同的风格构建，只是反过来。它由一系列密集层组成，这些层接收那个 32 维的潜在向量，并逐步将其扩展回去。最后的密集层将维度恢复到 120 乘 120 乘 3 的原始大小。

    注意这里的一个关键细节：对于解码器的最终输出层，我们使用了一个 Sigmoid 激活函数。我们为什么要这样做？嗯，在训练之前，我们对输入图像执行了标准的归一化步骤，以确保每一个原始像素值都被严格缩放到 0 和 1 之间。为了确保我们的重构图像也遵循这个有效的像素范围，我们使用了 Sigmoid 激活，它自然地将其输出限制在 0 和 1 之间。最后，在密集层生成这个长向量之后，我们简单地将其重塑回 120 乘 120 乘 3 的空间格式，以便它可以被解释和可视化为图像。

    尽管在概念上这是一个“简单”的密集自编码器，但如果你实际查看参数量，它是惊人的。因为输入层和输出层是如此庞大（43,200 个节点），将它们与内部密集层完全连接会导致大量的权重。它的编码器有将近五百万个参数，解码器也有五百万个，总共有大概一千万或一千一百万个参数。这是一个庞大的、计算量繁重的网络。训练设置没有什么特别之处；我们只是使用了标准的正则化和默认的 Adam 优化器。

    让我们训练它并看看结果。我们将首先在我们的测试集上评估训练好的自编码器。我们在顶行绘制原始的测试图像，在它们正下方，我们绘制自编码器的重构版本。老实说，看着它们，结果真的不怎么好。在人眼看来，它们就像非常模糊、褪色的颜色斑块。我不认为我们能从重构中自信地认出大多数的 Pokemon。很明显，我们应该能做得比这好得多。

    现在，你可能会想，这种糟糕的性能是否是由于巨大的泛化差距造成的——意思是，模型只是过度拟合了训练数据而在测试数据上失败了？为了检查这一点，我们在训练集的图像上评估完全相同的模型。当我们对比原始训练图像和它们的重构时，我们发现它在训练集上的表现一样糟糕。重构结果同样模糊和糟糕。因此，问题不在于泛化差距；问题在于这种密集的自编码器架构本身从根本上就不适合这项任务。正如我们在之前的讲座中讨论过的，核心问题是全连接神经网络根本不是处理空间图像数据的合适工具。

  - [15:10:45 - 15:15:28] **Building a Convolutional Autoencoder**

    We strongly suspect the poor performance is because we forced images through simple dense layers. Fully connected networks destroy the spatial structure of images the moment they flatten them. So, what we will do now is swap out those fully connected layers for Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) layers and see if we achieve better reconstruction results. We are still building a relatively simple model conceptually, just changing the mathematical operations.

    Let's look at the new CNN-based encoder first. It is easier to explain because it looks exactly like the front end of any standard image classification network you have built. We apply your usual convolutional layers, interspersed with average pooling layers to progressively downsample the spatial dimensions. If you trace the math, we start with an image size of 120 by 120. We apply an average pooling layer three separate times in the encoder. Each time we apply pooling, the spatial size—both height and width—is cut exactly in half. So, halving it three times means we are effectively dividing the original height and width by 2 to the power of 3, which is 8. Therefore, 120 divided by 8 gives us a final spatial resolution of 15 by 15. The number of channels, or feature maps, is determined by the number of filters in our final convolutional layer, which we set to 16. Therefore, the final output of our CNN encoder—our latent state—is a 3D tensor with dimensions 15 by 15 by 16.

    Now let's build the CNN-based decoder. The decoder will also utilize convolutional layers, but we face a specific architectural challenge. The decoder takes as input this small 15 by 15 by 16 tensor. In order to get back to the original 120 by 120 resolution, we need some specific layer that performs the exact opposite operation of the pooling layers we used in the encoder. We need an operation that increases the spatial size. In this basic demo, we are going to use something extremely simple, crude, and computationally cheap: a basic upsampling layer. All an upsampling layer does is take a single pixel value and duplicate it. If we are upsampling by a factor of 2, it takes one pixel and just blindly copies its value into a 2x2 grid (so 4 identical pixels). It doesn't learn anything; it just mathematically scales up the matrix. Every time we apply this upsampling layer, the image size doubles across both the height and the width. Because we applied pooling three times in the encoder, we must apply this upsampling step exactly three times in the decoder to perfectly reverse the spatial compression. Our final layer is a convolutional layer configured with exactly 3 filters. This ensures that the final output tensor shape is 120 by 120 by 3, perfectly matching the original RGB image format.

    If you multiply the dimensions of our latent tensor (15 * 15 * 16), it equals 3,600. Our original input was 43,200. So we are still achieving a very significant level of dimensionality reduction—compressing the data down to roughly 8% of its original size. Interestingly, if you look at the parameter count for this CNN-based model, it is actually quite small, substantially smaller than the 11 million parameter dense model. A big reason for this efficiency is that our upsampling method is deterministic; it just duplicates pixels and adds zero trainable parameters. Later on, we will discuss a more mathematically sound and "fancy" way to increase resolution, which is a core feature of the U-Net architecture. Instead of just dumbly copying pixels via upsampling, U-Net uses something called transposed convolutions, or strided convolutions, which is essentially a fully trainable version of upsampling that learns the best way to expand the image.

    But for now, let's stick with this simple convolutional autoencoder and train it on the Pokemon dataset. After training, let's again evaluate its performance on the test set. Here are the reconstructed images. They are still not perfect—they lack fine detail—but they are definitively, significantly better than the blurry blobs produced by the fully connected network. You can actually start to make out the distinct shapes and primary colors of the characters. And remember, it achieved this much better result using a model that is an order of magnitude smaller in terms of trainable parameters.

    我们强烈怀疑性能不佳是因为我们强行将图像输入到了简单的密集层中。全连接网络在将图像展平的那一刻就破坏了图像的空间结构。所以，我们现在要做的就是将那些全连接层替换为卷积神经网络 (CNN) 层，看看我们是否能获得更好的重构结果。从概念上讲，我们仍然在构建一个相对简单的模型，只是改变了数学运算。

    让我们先看看新的基于 CNN 的编码器。它比较容易解释，因为它看起来完全就像你构建过的任何标准图像分类网络的前端。我们应用通常的卷积层，并在其中穿插平均池化层，以逐步对空间维度进行下采样。如果你追踪一下数学计算，我们从 120 乘 120 的图像尺寸开始。我们在编码器中分别应用了三次平均池化层。每次我们应用池化时，空间大小——包括高度和宽度——都会被精确地减半。所以，减半三次意味着我们实际上是将原始的高度和宽度除以 2 的 3 次方，也就是 8。因此，120 除以 8 给出了 15 乘 15 的最终空间分辨率。通道数或特征图的数量由我们最后的卷积层中的滤波器数量决定，我们将其设置为 16。因此，我们的 CNN 编码器的最终输出——即我们的潜在状态——是一个尺寸为 15 乘 15 乘 16 的 3D 张量。

    现在让我们构建基于 CNN 的解码器。解码器也将利用卷积层，但我们面临一个特定的架构挑战。解码器接收这个小的 15 乘 15 乘 16 张量作为输入。为了恢复到原始的 120 乘 120 分辨率，我们需要一些特定的层来执行与我们在编码器中使用的池化层完全相反的操作。我们需要一种能增加空间尺寸的操作。在这个基础演示中，我们将使用一种极其简单、粗糙且计算成本低廉的东西：基本的上采样层。上采样层所做的只是提取单个像素值并将其复制。如果我们将图像上采样 2 倍，它提取一个像素，然后直接盲目地将其值复制到一个 2x2 的网格中（即 4 个相同的像素）。它没有学到任何东西；它只是在数学上放大了矩阵。每次我们应用这个上采样层时，图像大小在高度和宽度上都会翻倍。因为我们在编码器中应用了三次池化，所以我们必须在解码器中精确地应用这个上采样步骤三次，以完美地逆转空间压缩。我们的最后一层是配置了恰好 3 个滤波器的卷积层。这确保了最终的输出张量形状是 120 乘 120 乘 3，完美匹配原始的 RGB 图像格式。

    如果你把我们潜在张量的维度相乘 (15 * 15 * 16)，等于 3,600。我们原始的输入是 43,200。所以我们仍然实现了非常显著的降维水平——将数据压缩到大约其原始大小的 8%。有趣的是，如果你看看这个基于 CNN 的模型的参数量，它实际上非常小，大大少于那个 1100 万参数的密集模型。这种高效率的一个很大原因是我们的上采样方法是确定性的；它只是复制像素，并没有增加任何可训练参数。稍后，我们将讨论一种在数学上更合理、“更花哨”的提高分辨率的方法，这是 U-Net 架构的核心特征。U-Net 不仅仅是通过上采样盲目地复制像素，而是使用了一种叫做转置卷积（或跨步卷积）的东西，这本质上是上采样的完全可训练版本，它能学习扩展图像的最佳方式。

    但现在，让我们坚持使用这个简单的卷积自编码器，并在 Pokemon 数据集上训练它。训练结束后，让我们再次评估它在测试集上的性能。这是重构后的图像。它们仍然不够完美——缺乏微小的细节——但它们绝对、显著地比全连接网络产生的那种模糊斑块要好得多。你实际上已经开始能辨认出角色的独特形状和主要颜色。而且请记住，它使用了一个在可训练参数数量上小一个数量级的模型，却实现了好得多的结果。

  - [15:15:28 - 15:19:26] **Denoising Autoencoders using U-Net**

    So, our simple CNN autoencoder is better, but it's clearly still not that good. The model architecture we designed from scratch was just a bit too simplistic for high-quality image reconstruction. As I have emphasized before, in real-world machine learning practice, you rarely need to build complex models entirely from scratch. You look out into the literature, find highly successful, established models for your specific domain, and you either use them directly out of the box or make slight tweaks to suit your needs. For images, a remarkably successful autoencoder architecture is the U-Net. Instead of trying to invent a better network by hand, let's just utilize a standard U-Net implementation here. If you have the correct package installed that contains the U-Net architecture, you don't have to design the complex layers yourself; we can just instantiate a U-Net object.

    For this next part of the demo, instead of just standard compression, we are going to push the U-Net a bit further and attempt to train it as a denoising autoencoder. As we discussed theoretically before the break, our goal here is to train the U-Net specifically to take a noisy image and output a clean version, effectively reducing background noise. The mechanism for this involves deliberately feeding a artificially noise-perturbed version of our data into the U-Net as the input, while using the clean image as the target.

    To accomplish this efficiently during training, we treat the noise addition as a form of dynamic data augmentation. As we saw in previous lectures covering image pipelines, whenever you want to apply randomized transformations to your training data—like adding random noise—you should ideally use a data generator class, like Keras's `ImageDataGenerator`. This approach is far more memory-efficient because it applies the noise transformations "on the fly" as the data is being loaded in batches during training, rather than requiring you to pre-compute and store an entire noisy copy of your dataset in RAM. So, we instantiate an `ImageDataGenerator` class. For this specific denoising task, the augmentation is very straightforward: we are strictly just adding random Gaussian noise to the pixels. However, there is one subtle but critical implementation detail you must handle when adding noise mathematically. When you add random Gaussian values to your pixel matrices, some pixel values might be pushed below 0 or above 1. Because valid normalized image pixels must lie exactly in the [0, 1] interval, you must apply a clipping function immediately after adding the noise to strictly bound the resulting values between 0 and 1.

    So, we initialize our U-Net model—you can review the intricate architectural details of its skip connections later in your own time. We then set up the data pipeline where we inject Gaussian noise dynamically using the `ImageDataGenerator` to create a flow object. This flow object essentially replaces our standard static `x` input array when we call the `fit` function to train the model.

    After training, let's examine the performance of our denoising U-Net on the test set. I've arranged the visual output into three distinct rows. The top row displays the original, perfectly clean test images. The middle row shows the corrupted versions—these are the images after we have heavily perturbed them with Gaussian noise. This is what we actually feed into the trained U-Net model for inference. The bottom row displays the final output predicted by the U-Net. Looking at the results, I think it is doing a quite respectable job. It's not bad at all. It successfully learns to identify and effectively smooth out the artificial background noise while largely preserving the underlying structure of the original image.

    As I mentioned earlier, it is highly worthwhile to study the U-Net architecture in your own time. Conceptually, it shares similarities with the simple convolutional autoencoder we built, but its power comes from key architectural differences. Primarily, its upsampling path relies on fully trainable strided convolutions rather than simple pixel duplication, and crucially, it utilizes "skip connections" that directly pass high-resolution feature maps from the encoder stages straight across to the corresponding decoder stages, which massively helps in reconstructing fine spatial details.

    Are there any questions about the autoencoder demo before we move on? Okay, if not, let's proceed to the next topic.

    所以，我们简单的 CNN 自编码器更好一些，但显然仍然不够好。我们从头开始设计的模型架构对于高质量的图像重构来说有点过于简单了。正如我之前强调的那样，在现实世界的机器学习实践中，你很少需要完全从零开始构建复杂的模型。你去查阅文献，找到在你的特定领域内非常成功、成熟的模型，你可以直接开箱即用，或者进行轻微的调整以适应你的需求。对于图像而言，一个非常成功的自编码器架构是 U-Net。与其试图手动发明一个更好的网络，不如让我们直接在这里使用标准的 U-Net 实现。如果你安装了包含 U-Net 架构的正确软件包，你就不必自己设计复杂的层；我们只需实例化一个 U-Net 对象即可。

    在演示的下一部分，我们不仅要进行标准的压缩，我们还要将 U-Net 推进一点，尝试将其训练为一个去噪自编码器。正如我们在休息前从理论上讨论的那样，我们这里的目标是专门训练 U-Net 接收嘈杂的图像并输出干净的版本，从而有效地减少背景噪声。实现这一目标的机制包括故意将我们数据的人工添加噪声的版本输入到 U-Net 作为输入，同时使用干净的图像作为目标。

    为了在训练期间有效地完成这项工作，我们将添加噪声视为动态数据增强的一种形式。正如我们在之前涵盖图像管道的讲座中看到的，每当你想要对训练数据应用随机变换时——比如添加随机噪声——你最好使用一个数据生成器类，比如 Keras 的 `ImageDataGenerator`。这种方法在内存上要高效得多，因为它在训练期间分批加载数据时“实时”应用噪声变换，而不是要求你预先计算并在 RAM 中存储数据集的整个嘈杂副本。所以，我们实例化了一个 `ImageDataGenerator` 类。对于这个特定的去噪任务，数据增强非常直接：我们严格地只是向像素添加随机的高斯噪声。然而，在数学上添加噪声时，你必须处理一个微妙但关键的实现细节。当你在像素矩阵中加入随机的高斯值时，一些像素值可能会被推到 0 以下或 1 以上。因为有效的、归一化的图像像素必须严格处于 [0, 1] 区间内，你必须在添加噪声后立即应用截断函数，严格将结果值限制在 0 和 1 之间。

    因此，我们初始化了 U-Net 模型——你以后可以在自己的时间里复习其跳跃连接的复杂架构细节。然后我们设置数据管道，我们使用 `ImageDataGenerator` 动态注入高斯噪声来创建一个流对象。当我们调用 `fit` 函数来训练模型时，这个流对象本质上取代了我们标准静态的 `x` 输入数组。

    训练结束后，让我们检查一下我们的去噪 U-Net 在测试集上的性能。我将视觉输出分成了不同的三行。顶行显示了原始的、完全干净的测试图像。中间一行显示了损坏的版本——这是我们在用高斯噪声对它们进行严重干扰后的图像。这就是我们在推理时实际输入到训练好的 U-Net 模型中的内容。底行显示了 U-Net 预测的最终输出。看看结果，我认为它做得相当不错。一点也不差。它成功地学会了识别并有效地平滑人工背景噪声，同时在很大程度上保留了原始图像的底层结构。

    正如我之前提到的，在你们自己的时间里研究 U-Net 架构是非常值得的。在概念上，它与我们构建的简单卷积自编码器有相似之处，但它的强大之处在于关键的架构差异。主要是，它的上采样路径依赖于完全可训练的跨步卷积，而不是简单的像素复制，并且至关重要的是，它利用了“跳跃连接”，将高分辨率特征图从编码器阶段直接传递到相应的解码器阶段，这极大地有助于重构精细的空间细节。

    在我们继续之前，关于自编码器演示还有什么问题吗？好的，如果没有，让我们进入下一个主题。

  - [15:19:26 - 15:26:47] **Semi-Supervised Learning and its Core Assumptions**

    Okay, so we have now thoroughly looked at unsupervised learning, specifically focusing our attention on dimensionality reduction techniques. Now let's pivot and look at semi-supervised learning. Conceptually, semi-supervised learning sits somewhere directly between pure supervised learning and pure unsupervised learning.

    It addresses a very specific, prevalent problem we face constantly in practical, real-world machine learning: getting highly accurate, human-labeled data is incredibly difficult and expensive. Because of this, you will almost universally find yourself in a situation where you possess a massive amount of unlabeled data alongside a very small amount of labeled data. Think about the logistics: you can collect huge volumes of unlabeled data automatically without needing any specialized domain knowledge. For example, writing a web scraper to download millions of random pictures of dogs or scraping publicly available X-ray images from a database is computationally trivial. However, creating a labeled dataset from those images requires human intervention. If you are dealing with something complex like medical X-rays, you can't just ask anyone; you need an actual, highly-trained radiologist to sit down and professionally annotate every single image. That is a very slow and extremely expensive bottleneck.

    So, supervised machine learning explicitly deals with this exact scenario: you have a large unlabeled dataset, which we will call D_U, and a much smaller labeled dataset, which we call D_L. In standard supervised learning, you completely ignore D_U because you only use data where you have both the input x and the label y. In semi-supervised learning, we attempt to leverage both D_L and D_U simultaneously. The goal is to see if that vast pool of unlabeled data can somehow improve our model's performance on a specific task. We often divide semi-supervised learning into two broad types of problems. The first is the transductive problem. In a transductive setting, your goal is narrowly defined: you are simply trying to train a model to accurately infer and assign labels exclusively to the specific, known examples within your provided unlabeled dataset D_U. For example, a hospital hands you a specific, finite batch of medical X-rays, and your only job is to determine the class labels for that exact batch. The second is the inductive problem. The inductive model represents a much more general and ambitious task. Here, the goal is to leverage both D_L and D_U to learn a general, universally applicable function (f-star) that can then be deployed to make accurate predictions on completely new, unseen input samples that were never part of either dataset. For instance, building a general-purpose object detection system for a self-driving car using a mix of labeled and unlabeled data. In this course, we will primarily focus more on the transductive model, as the inductive one is considerably more complex.

    But this brings up a fundamental question: how exactly can a dataset with absolutely no labels actually help you when your ultimate goal is to predict a specific label? How does unlabeled data provide actionable information? Let's consider a simple binary classification thought experiment. Suppose you have exactly two labeled data points in a 2D space: one is a purple dot (which we will say is Class 1) and the other is an orange dot (which is Class 2). If those two dots are all the information you have, the most mathematically reasonable decision boundary to draw is a simple straight line situated exactly halfway between them, perfectly splitting the space.

    However, suppose I now reveal a large cluster of unlabeled data points in the same space. You don't know their class, but you see their geometric distribution. You notice a dense clump of these unlabeled points immediately surrounding the orange dot, stretching off to the right. Looking at this new spatial distribution, where do you intuitively think the decision boundary should be drawn now? It visually feels like the boundary should shift significantly to the left, closer to the isolated purple dot, effectively grouping that entire dense cluster of unlabeled points together with the orange point.

    Why does this shifted decision boundary look intuitively correct and mathematically pleasing to us? It is because our human brains are subconsciously applying specific structural assumptions about how data behaves. The primary assumption we are making here is what we call the continuity assumption. The continuity assumption formally states that data points which are geometrically close to each other in the feature space are highly likely to share the exact same label. Therefore, because all those unlabeled points are sitting right next to the known orange point, we assume they are also Class 2. Conversely, points clustered near the purple point would be assumed to be Class 1. By implicitly assigning these inferred labels based on proximity, the unlabeled data drastically alters our perception of the classes' shapes and sizes. It dictates that it is far more reasonable to assume the entire dense structure belongs to Class 2, and so we confidently shift our boundary to accommodate that structure. Thus, unlabeled data provides highly valuable structural information, but strictly only if you accept certain underlying mathematical assumptions.

    Let's clearly define the three most common assumptions that underpin all semi-supervised learning algorithms.

    1. First is the Continuity Assumption, which we just discussed: points close to each other are likely to have the same label.
    2. Second is the Cluster Assumption. This is closely related but conceptually distinct. It assumes that data naturally organizes itself into discrete, high-density clusters separated by low-density valleys. The assumption dictates that all points belonging to the same continuous cluster should share the same class label.
    3. Third is the Manifold Assumption, which approaches the problem from a dimensionality reduction perspective. It assumes that while the data may exist in a massively high-dimensional space, the actual relevant information lies perfectly upon a much lower-dimensional curve or surface—a manifold—embedded within that space. Locally, the space around the manifold behaves simply.

    To visually summarize: The continuity assumption links close points to known labels. The cluster assumption assigns labels based on distinct geometric groupings. And the manifold assumption traces how data continuously varies along a specific low-dimensional structure, even in a high-dimensional space. If you flat-out refuse to make at least one of these assumptions, the unlabeled data is mathematically useless to you for prediction tasks.

    好的，我们现在已经彻底地研究了无监督学习，特别是将注意力集中在降维技术上。现在让我们转移方向，来看看半监督学习。从概念上讲，半监督学习正好处于纯监督学习和纯无监督学习之间。

    它解决了一个我们在实际的、现实世界的机器学习中经常面临的非常具体且普遍的问题：获取高度准确的、人工标记的数据是非常困难和昂贵的。正因为如此，你几乎会普遍发现自己处于这样一种情况：你拥有海量的未标记数据，而只有极少量的标记数据。想想其中的逻辑：你可以在不需要任何专业领域知识的情况下，自动收集大量的未标记数据。例如，编写一个网络爬虫来下载数百万张随机的狗的图片，或者从数据库中抓取公开的 X 光图像，在计算上是非常简单的。然而，从这些图像中创建一个带有标记的数据集需要人工干预。如果你处理的是像医疗 X 光片这样复杂的东西，你不能随便找个人来做；你需要一个真正的、受过高度训练的放射科医生坐下来，专业地标注每一张图像。这是一个非常缓慢且极其昂贵的瓶颈。

    因此，半监督机器学习明确处理的正是这种场景：你有一个大型的未标记数据集，我们称之为 D_U，以及一个小得多的标记数据集，我们称之为 D_L。在标准的监督学习中，你完全忽略 D_U，因为你只使用同时具有输入 x 和标签 y 的数据。在半监督学习中，我们试图同时利用 D_L 和 D_U。目标是看看那庞大的未标记数据池是否能以某种方式提高我们的模型在特定任务上的性能。我们通常将半监督学习分为两大类问题。第一类是直推式问题。在直推式设定中，你的目标被狭义地定义：你只是试图训练一个模型，准确地为你提供的未标记数据集 D_U 中特定、已知的样本推断并分配标签。例如，一家医院交给你一批特定、数量有限的医疗 X 光片，你唯一的工作就是确定那批 X 光片的类别标签。第二类是归纳式问题。归纳式模型代表了一个更普遍、更具野心的任务。在这里，目标是同时利用 D_L 和 D_U 来学习一个通用的、普遍适用的函数（f-star），该函数随后可以部署以对完全新的、未见过的输入样本做出准确的预测，而这些样本从未属于任何一个数据集。例如，使用标记和未标记数据的混合，为自动驾驶汽车构建一个通用的对象检测系统。在这门课程中，我们将主要关注直推式模型，因为归纳式模型要复杂得多。

    但这引出了一个根本问题：当你的最终目标是预测一个特定的标签时，一个完全没有标签的数据集究竟能如何帮助你？未标记数据是如何提供可操作信息的？让我们考虑一个简单的二元分类思想实验。假设你在 2D 空间中正好有两个标记的数据点：一个是紫色的点（我们说是类别 1），另一个是橙色的点（也就是类别 2）。如果这两个点就是你拥有的所有信息，那么在数学上最合理的决策边界是在它们之间正中间画一条简单的直线，将空间完美地一分为二。

    然而，假设我现在在同一个空间中展示了一大群未标记的数据点。你不知道它们的类别，但你看到了它们的几何分布。你注意到这群未标记的点紧紧围绕着橙色的点，并向右延伸。看着这个新的空间分布，你直觉上认为现在决策边界应该画在哪里？视觉上感觉边界应该显著向左偏移，靠近那个孤立的紫色点，有效地将整个密集的未标记点簇与橙色点归为一组。

    为什么这个偏移的决策边界在直觉上看起来是正确的，并且让我们在数学上感到满意呢？这是因为我们的大脑在潜意识中应用了关于数据行为方式的特定结构假设。我们在这里做出的主要假设被称为连续性假设。连续性假设正式声明：在特征空间中几何距离相互靠近的数据点极有可能共享完全相同的标签。因此，因为所有那些未标记的点都紧挨着已知的橙色点，我们假设它们也是类别 2。相反，聚集在紫色点附近的数据点将被假定为类别 1。通过基于邻近性隐式地分配这些推断的标签，未标记数据极大地改变了我们对类别形状和大小的感知。它表明，假设整个密集结构属于类别 2 要合理得多，所以我们自信地移动我们的边界以适应那个结构。因此，未标记数据提供了极具价值的结构信息，但这严格地取决于你是否接受某些潜在的数学假设。

    让我们清楚地定义支撑所有半监督学习算法的三个最常见的假设。

    1. 首先是我们刚刚讨论过的连续性假设：彼此靠近的点很可能具有相同的标签。
    2. 其次是聚类假设。这密切相关但在概念上截然不同。它假设数据自然地组织成由低密度山谷分隔的离散、高密度簇。该假设规定属于同一连续簇的所有点都应共享相同的类别标签。
    3. 第三是流形假设，它从降维的角度处理问题。它假设虽然数据可能存在于一个极其高维的空间中，但实际相关的信息完美地位于该空间内嵌的一个低得多的维度曲线或表面——流形——上。在局部范围内，流形周围的空间表现得很简单。

    为了在视觉上进行总结：连续性假设将靠近的点与已知标签联系起来。聚类假设基于截然不同的几何分组来分配标签。流形假设则追踪数据即使在高维空间中也是如何沿着特定的低维结构连续变化的。如果你断然拒绝至少做出这些假设之一，那么未标记数据在预测任务中对你来说在数学上就是毫无用处的。

  - [15:26:47 - 15:39:57] **The Self-Training Algorithm**

    So, assuming we buy into these structural assumptions, what are the actual algorithms we can code to perform semi-supervised learning? The most fundamentally simple algorithm in this space is called self-training. The core idea behind self-training is wonderfully straightforward: you use an initially, partially trained model to predict pseudo-labels for your unlabeled data. You then treat those predictions as ground truth, feed them back into the model, and train it again recursively.

    Let's break down the basic self-training algorithm step-by-step:

    1. **Step 1:** You completely ignore the unlabeled dataset temporarily. You take your small labeled dataset (D_L) and fully train an initial model, let's call it f-hat, using standard supervised learning techniques.
    2. **Step 2:** You take a sample of data points from your massive unlabeled dataset (D_U). You pass these points through your trained f-hat model and ask it to predict a label for each one, giving you y-hat = f-hat(x-tilde).
    3. **Step 3:** You take these newly generated input-prediction pairs (x-tilde, y-hat) and permanently add them to your labeled dataset D_L. You then loop completely back to Step 1 and retrain a brand new f-hat model from scratch on this newly augmented, larger D_L dataset.

    When implementing this, you have significant engineering freedom in how you handle steps 2 and 3. The most critical decision is determining which unlabeled points you actually trust enough to add to D_L. You don't have to add all of them. A very common and robust strategy is to only add the predictions that the model is highly confident about. How do you measure a model's confidence? It depends on the architecture. If your model outputs a probability vector (like a softmax layer), you might only select instances where the highest predicted class probability exceeds a strict threshold, say 0.95. If you are using a Support Vector Machine (SVM), the mathematical distance from the point to the decision boundary is an excellent proxy for confidence—points very far away from the margin are confident predictions. You could configure the algorithm to only add the top 10 most confident predictions per iteration, or maybe the top 10 percent. Alternatively, you could choose a more aggressive route and add every single prediction to D_L, but instead of treating them as absolute truth, you assign each added point a statistical weight. The weight is mathematically tied to the confidence score—perhaps derived from the entropy of the prediction—meaning less confident pseudo-labels exert far less influence on the gradient during the next round of training.

    Let's walk through a highly simplified, concrete 1D mathematical example to visually demonstrate how self-training actually shifts boundaries. Imagine a 1D feature space along the x-axis. We are given a tiny labeled dataset consisting of exactly two points. At x = -1, we have a point explicitly labeled Class 0. At x = 2, we have a point explicitly labeled Class 1. We are also given a small unlabeled dataset with two points located at x = -1 (overlapping the known point) and x = 1.

    So, we execute Step 1: Train an initial model on the labeled data. We only have points at -1 and 2. If we train a basic linear classifier like an SVM, the mathematically optimal maximum-margin decision boundary is exactly at the midpoint between them. The distance between -1 and 2 is 3, so half of that is 1.5. Adding 1.5 to -1 places our initial decision boundary exactly at x = 0.5. Our initial model, f-hat_1, defines Class 1 as anything where x is greater than 0.5, and Class 0 as anything less.

    Now we execute Step 2: Use this model to predict labels for the unlabeled data points at x = -1 and x = 1. For the point at x = 1, since 1 is greater than 0.5, the model strictly predicts Class 1. For the point at x = -1, since -1 is less than 0.5, the model strictly predicts Class 0.

    Finally, Step 3: We formally augment our dataset. We take those newly labeled points and merge them with our original training data. Our new, augmented dataset now consists of four points: our original (-1, Class 0) and (2, Class 1), plus our new pseudo-labeled points (-1, Class 0) and (1, Class 1).

    Now we loop back to Step 1 and retrain the SVM on this new dataset. Look at the new spatial distribution: we have Class 0 points clustered down at x = -1, and we have Class 1 points at x = 1 and x = 2. To maximize the margin between these two distinct groups, the SVM will recalculate the boundary to fall exactly halfway between the innermost points of each class. The boundary must now sit halfway between x = -1 (the highest Class 0) and x = 1 (the lowest Class 1). Therefore, the new boundary fundamentally shifts to exactly x = 0. Our new model, f-hat_2, classifies points as Class 1 if x > 0. Through the iterative process of self-training, the model effectively utilized the spatial distribution of the unlabeled data to dramatically pull the decision boundary to a more logical location.

    The primary advantage of self-training is that it is remarkably simple to implement. Furthermore, it acts as a "meta-algorithm," meaning it is completely agnostic to the underlying model architecture. You can wrap a self-training loop around an SVM, a random forest, or a massive deep neural network without changing the core logic. However, its greatest disadvantage is a catastrophic failure mode: if your initial model is poorly trained and generates confident but incorrect pseudo-labels, adding those false labels back into the training set will poison the model. The model will actively reinforce its own early mistakes, causing the decision boundary to drift further and further away from reality. Furthermore, from an academic standpoint, self-training algorithms generally lack rigorous theoretical guarantees regarding their convergence or optimality; they remain primarily a powerful empirical heuristic.

    因此，假设我们接受这些结构性假设，那么我们可以编写哪些实际算法来执行半监督学习呢？在这个领域中，最基本、最简单的算法称为自训练。自训练背后的核心思想非常直接：你使用一个最初部分训练好的模型，为你的未标记数据预测伪标签。然后你将这些预测视为真实情况，将它们反馈给模型，并递归地再次训练它。

    让我们逐步分解基本的自训练算法：

    1. **第一步：** 你暂时完全忽略未标记数据集。你拿起你那小巧的标记数据集 (D_L)，并使用标准的监督学习技术充分训练一个初始模型，我们称之为 f-hat。
    2. **第二步：** 你从海量的未标记数据集 (D_U) 中抽取一部分数据点样本。你将这些点通过你训练好的 f-hat 模型，并要求它为每个点预测一个标签，从而得到 y-hat = f-hat(x-tilde)。
    3. **第三步：** 你提取这些新生成的输入-预测对 (x-tilde, y-hat)，并将它们永久地添加到你的标记数据集 D_L 中。然后你完全循环回到第一步，在这个新增强的、更大的 D_L 数据集上从头开始重新训练一个全新的 f-hat 模型。

    在实现这个算法时，你在处理第 2 步和第 3 步的方式上有很大的工程自由度。最关键的决定是确定你真正信任哪些未标记点，以将它们添加到 D_L 中。你不必把它们全部加进去。一个非常常见且稳健的策略是，只添加模型具有高度置信度的预测。你如何衡量模型的置信度？这取决于架构。如果你的模型输出一个概率向量（比如 Softmax 层），你可能只选择最高预测类别概率超过严格阈值（比如 0.95）的实例。如果你使用的是支持向量机 (SVM)，那么点到决策边界的数学距离就是置信度的一个极佳替代指标——距离边界非常远的点就是置信度高的预测。你可以配置算法每次迭代只添加置信度排名前 10 的预测，或者可能是前 10%。或者，你可以选择一条更激进的路线，将每一个预测都添加到 D_L 中，但不要将它们视为绝对的真理，而是为每个添加的点分配一个统计权重。这个权重在数学上与置信度分数（也许源自预测的熵）挂钩——这意味着不太自信的伪标签在下一轮训练中对梯度的影响要小得多。

    让我们通过一个高度简化的、具体的 1D 数学例子来看看自训练实际上是如何移动边界的。想象一个沿着 x 轴的 1D 特征空间。我们得到一个微小的标记数据集，恰好包含两个点。在 x = -1 处，我们有一个明确标记为类别 0 的点。在 x = 2 处，我们有一个明确标记为类别 1 的点。我们还得到一个小的未标记数据集，包含两个点，分别位于 x = -1（与已知点重叠）和 x = 1。

    所以，我们执行第一步：在标记数据上训练一个初始模型。我们只有 -1 和 2 处的点。如果我们训练一个基本的线性分类器（比如 SVM），数学上最佳的最大间隔决策边界正好在它们的中点。-1 和 2 之间的距离是 3，所以一半是 1.5。把 1.5 加到 -1 上，我们的初始决策边界就正好位于 x = 0.5。我们的初始模型 f-hat_1 将类别 1 定义为 x 大于 0.5 的任何点，将类别 0 定义为 x 小于 0.5 的任何点。

    现在我们执行第二步：使用这个模型为 x = -1 和 x = 1 处的未标记数据点预测标签。对于 x = 1 处的点，因为 1 大于 0.5，模型严格预测为类别 1。对于 x = -1 处的点，因为 -1 小于 0.5，模型严格预测为类别 0。

    最后，第三步：我们正式增强我们的数据集。我们拿起那些新标记的点，并将它们与我们最初的训练数据合并。我们新的、增强的数据集现在包含四个点：我们最初的 (-1, 类别 0) 和 (2, 类别 1)，加上我们新的带伪标签的点 (-1, 类别 0) 和 (1, 类别 1)。

    现在我们循环回到第一步，并在这个新数据集上重新训练 SVM。看看新的空间分布：我们在 x = -1 处聚集了类别 0 的点，我们在 x = 1 和 x = 2 处有类别 1 的点。为了最大化这两个不同组之间的间隔，SVM 将重新计算边界，使其正好落在每个类别最内侧点的一半位置。边界现在必须位于 x = -1（最大的类别 0）和 x = 1（最小的类别 1）之间的一半处。因此，新边界从根本上移动到了准确的 x = 0 处。我们的新模型 f-hat_2 将 x > 0 的点分类为类别 1。通过自训练的迭代过程，模型有效地利用了未标记数据的空间分布，戏剧性地将决策边界拉到了一个更合理的位置。

    自训练的主要优势在于它实现起来非常简单。此外，它充当了一个“元算法”，这意味着它完全独立于底层的模型架构。你可以将自训练循环包装在 SVM、随机森林或庞大的深度神经网络周围，而无需改变核心逻辑。然而，它最大的缺点是一个灾难性的故障模式：如果你的初始模型训练不佳并生成了自信但不正确的伪标签，将这些错误的标签加回训练集将会毒害模型。模型将积极地强化自己早期的错误，导致决策边界越来越偏离现实。此外，从学术的角度来看，自训练算法在收敛性或最优性方面通常缺乏严格的理论保证；它们主要还是一种强大的经验启发式方法。

  - [15:39:57 - 15:44:01] **Label Propagation**

    Beyond self-training, another highly popular and distinct method for semi-supervised learning is called label propagation. To understand why we need this, look at this slide showing two handwritten number '2's from the MNIST dataset. They are written by completely different people. If you take the raw pixel arrays of these two specific images and compare them mathematically—perhaps by calculating the L2 distance or taking a dot product—they don't look mathematically similar at all. Without labels, an algorithm would struggle to classify them in the same group based purely on direct comparison.

    However, if we populate the space between them with a large dataset of other handwritten '2's from the unlabeled set, a fascinating structure emerges. You can arrange these intermediate images such that each adjacent pair looks incredibly similar to one another. The visual variation—say, the size of the loop at the bottom of the '2'—changes only slightly from one image to the next in the sequence. Even though the two images at the extreme ends are radically different, they are securely connected by a dense chain of "stepping stones." This is a perfect, textbook illustration of the manifold assumption in practice. The underlying manifold represents the concept of a '2', and the data smoothly varies along its surface across different handwriting styles.

    Label propagation exploits exactly this structure. The algorithm works by literally propagating the known label from a labeled starting point, step-by-step across these tight similarities, to label the entire manifold. If image A (with a big loop) is a '2', and image B (with a slightly smaller loop) is nearly identical to A, it's highly reasonable to assign the label '2' to B. You then propagate the label from B to C, and so on. By taking it one small step at a time along the dense regions of data, you can confidently traverse the entire manifold and eventually deduce that the drastically different image with no loop is definitively the same class as the one with a massive loop.

    There are many mathematical variations of the specific label propagation algorithm, but the fundamental mechanics generally follow these steps:

    1. **Step 1:** You must first construct a mathematical graph representing your entire dataset (both labeled and unlabeled). In this graph, every single data point is a node. The edges connecting the nodes represent the degree of closeness or similarity between them. Crucially, because distances in raw, high-dimensional pixel space become mathematically meaningless (due to the curse of dimensionality), you often must pass the data through a dimensionality reduction step first—perhaps using an autoencoder—and calculate the edge weights based on distances in the low-dimensional latent space.
    2. **Step 2:** You process the nodes in a randomized order. When you evaluate an unlabeled node, you look at all of its immediate, connected neighbors in the graph. You determine what the majority label is among those surrounding neighbors, and you explicitly assign that majority label to the current node.
    3. **Step 3:** You evaluate the state of the network. You stop the algorithm entirely only if the system reaches equilibrium—meaning every single node in the entire graph possesses a label that perfectly matches the majority label of its neighbors. If this condition is not perfectly met, you loop back to Step 2, randomize the processing order of the nodes again, and run another complete sweep of label assignments.

    If you visualize this process running on a complex, swirly 2D dataset with distinct classes, you will initially just see a few colored triangles representing the known labels amidst a sea of gray circles representing the unlabeled data. As the algorithm iterates, you will visually see the colors "flow" or propagate outward from the labeled points along the dense pathways of the manifold, slowly colorizing the gray circles until the entire contiguous structure shares the correct label. The resulting size of the circles in these visualizations often represents the mathematical confidence of the propagated label. You can read up on the original papers for the rigorous mathematical proofs underlying this process.

    除了自训练之外，另一种非常流行且截然不同的半监督学习方法被称为标签传播。要理解为什么我们需要这个，看看这张幻灯片，上面显示了 MNIST 数据集中的两个手写数字“2”。它们是由完全不同的人写的。如果你提取这两个特定图像的原始像素数组并在数学上对它们进行比较——也许通过计算 L2 距离或取点积——它们在数学上看起来根本不相似。如果没有标签，算法将很难纯粹基于直接比较将它们归为同一类。

    然而，如果我们在它们之间的空间中填充大量来自未标记集的其他手写“2”的数据，就会出现一个迷人的结构。你可以排列这些中间图像，使得每对相邻的图像看起来都极其相似。视觉变化——比如，“2”底部环的大小——在序列中从一张图像到下一张图像只是轻微地改变。尽管处于极端的两张图像截然不同，但它们被密集的“垫脚石”链牢固地连接在一起。这是流形假设在实践中的一个完美的教科书式说明。潜在的流形代表了“2”的概念，数据在不同书写风格中沿着其表面平滑地变化。

    标签传播正是利用了这种结构。该算法的工作原理是从标记的起点开始，一步步地沿着这些紧密的相似性传播已知标签，从而标记整个流形。如果图像 A（带有一个大环）是“2”，并且图像 B（带有一个稍小的环）与 A 几乎相同，那么将标签“2”分配给 B 是非常合理的。然后你将标签从 B 传播到 C，依此类推。通过沿着数据的密集区域每次前进一小步，你可以自信地遍历整个流形，并最终推断出没有环的截然不同的图像与有巨大环的图像绝对属于同一类别。

    特定的标签传播算法有许多数学变体，但基本机制通常遵循以下步骤：

    1. **第一步：** 你必须首先构建一个数学图来表示你的整个数据集（包括标记的和未标记的）。在这个图中，每一个数据点都是一个节点。连接节点的边代表它们之间的接近或相似程度。至关重要的是，因为在原始的、高维的像素空间中的距离变得在数学上毫无意义（由于维度灾难），你通常必须首先将数据通过一个降维步骤——也许使用自编码器——并基于低维潜在空间中的距离计算边权重。
    2. **第二步：** 你以随机的顺序处理节点。当你评估一个未标记的节点时，你会查看图中它所有直接连接的邻居。你确定那些周围邻居中的多数标签是什么，并将该多数标签明确地分配给当前节点。
    3. **第三步：** 你评估网络的状态。只有当系统达到平衡状态——意味着整个图中的每一个节点都拥有一个完美匹配其邻居的多数标签的标签时，你才完全停止算法。如果这个条件没有完美满足，你循环回到第二步，再次随机化节点的处理顺序，并运行另一轮完整的标签分配。

    如果你将这个过程在一个带有明显类别的复杂、漩涡状 2D 数据集上可视化运行，你最初只会看到几个代表已知标签的彩色三角形位于代表未标记数据的灰色圆圈的海洋中。随着算法的迭代，你将在视觉上看到颜色沿着流形的密集路径从标记点向外“流动”或传播，慢慢地给灰色圆圈着色，直到整个连续的结构共享正确的标签。在这些可视化中，圆圈的最终大小通常代表传播标签的数学置信度。你可以阅读原始论文，以了解支撑这一过程的严谨数学证明。

  - [15:44:01 - 15:52:12] **Multi-Task Learning and Transfer Learning**

    Let's transition away from semi-supervised learning. I want to spend the rest of the lecture discussing a massive and incredibly practical topic: Multi-Task Learning, or learning across different tasks.

    Multi-task learning is an architectural paradigm concerned with training models to perform more than one distinct task simultaneously, or critically, learning common, reusable representations that benefit multiple different tasks. It operates on the premise that you can leverage useful information contained within the training signals of related tasks to improve the generalization performance of all the tasks. We generally divide this into two broad conceptual approaches: Transfer Learning and Domain Adaptation. Transfer learning handles situations where the input data across different models is broadly similar in nature, but the specific output tasks you want the models to perform are completely different. Domain adaptation deals with the exact reverse: the output task is completely identical, but the mathematical distribution of the input data changes significantly between domains.

    For any cross-task learning to function, we are leaning heavily on the manifold assumption once again. The core theoretical premise is that if the input data for two different tasks shares similar structural characteristics, then the features necessary to solve both tasks likely lie on the exact same low-dimensional manifold. For instance, if you have two entirely separate datasets of text, one for sentiment analysis and one for spam detection, the foundational understanding of the language itself—how syntax works, what words mean contextually—is universal. Therefore, the low-dimensional word embeddings representing the language manifold should be perfectly reusable for both tasks. In deep learning architectures, this means that the early layers of a neural network—which act as feature extractors that map complex raw data down onto that simpler manifold—can and absolutely should be shared across different models. You only need to specialize the final, later layers to adapt those common features to the specific distinct tasks.

    This underlying theory leads directly to transfer learning. In transfer learning, the data types are similar, but the ultimate tasks are disparate. Because the data types are fundamentally similar, we logically assume that the different tasks rely on extracting identical low-level features. How do we implement this practically? One method is "Sharing Hidden States." Imagine an architecture where a single input 'x' is fed into a neural network. The initial layers are explicitly shared, processing 'x' and generating a "shared hidden state"—a common pool of foundational features. For image processing, these shared layers learn to detect universal geometric patterns like lines, edges, and simple shapes. From this central shared hub, the network splits into separate branches. Task 1 might branch off to perform facial recognition to identify a person, while Task 2 branches off to perform emotion classification to determine if they are happy. Both tasks completely rely on the exact same foundational feature set extracted by the shared early layers. Conversely, you can have architectures with multiple distinct input streams converging on a common output task. For example, predicting a person's sentiment based on a text stream from their blog and an independent video stream from their vlog. Both independent streams are processed by dedicated feature extractors, and those extracted features are subsequently merged into a shared final network to make the single prediction.

    By far, the most universally utilized technique in modern machine learning is transfer learning via pre-trained networks. The methodology is straightforward and immensely powerful. You take a massive, extremely deep convolutional neural network and you exhaustively train it on a gargantuan dataset for a specific task. A classic example is training a CNN on the ImageNet dataset, which contains millions of highly diverse images. We will call this Task 1. Because it was trained on such a vast amount of data, this network has been forced to learn incredibly robust, generalized feature extraction capabilities.

    Now, suppose you have your own specific, niche problem—say, classifying types of microscopic cancer cells—and you only possess a very small dataset for it. This is Task 2. Instead of initializing a new neural network with random weights and trying to train it from scratch on your tiny dataset (which would almost certainly fail or overfit massively), you leverage the pre-trained model. You take the weights learned from Task 1 and use them to explicitly "warm-start" your new model for Task 2.

    Practically, this means you take the architecture of the pre-trained model and copy its learned weights directly into your new model. Because the final output layer of the pre-trained model is specific to ImageNet's 1000 classes, you cut off that final layer and replace it with a newly initialized dense layer specifically sized for your cancer cell classes. When you begin training on your small dataset, your model doesn't have to spend epochs figuring out how to detect basic edges and textures—it already knows how from the ImageNet weights. Depending on the exact scenario, you might choose to "freeze" the weights of the early convolutional layers, meaning you completely prevent them from updating during training and strictly only allow backpropagation to adjust your new, customized final layers. This ensures the foundational features remain perfectly preserved while the final layers adapt them to the new task.

    让我们从半监督学习过渡开来。我想在讲座的剩余时间里讨论一个庞大且极其有用的话题：多任务学习，或者说跨不同任务的学习。

    多任务学习是一种架构范式，涉及训练模型同时执行多个不同的任务，或者更关键的是，学习有益于多个不同任务的通用、可重用的表示。它的运作前提是，你可以利用相关任务的训练信号中包含的有用信息来提高所有任务的泛化性能。我们通常将此分为两种广泛的概念方法：迁移学习和领域自适应。迁移学习处理不同模型间的输入数据在性质上大体相似，但你希望模型执行的具体输出任务完全不同的情况。领域自适应处理的正好相反：输出任务完全相同，但输入数据的数学分布在领域之间发生了显著变化。

    为了使任何跨任务学习发挥作用，我们再次严重依赖流形假设。核心理论前提是，如果两个不同任务的输入数据具有相似的结构特征，那么解决这两个任务所需的特征很可能完全位于同一个低维流形上。例如，如果你有两个完全独立的文本数据集，一个用于情感分析，一个用于垃圾邮件检测，那么对语言本身的基础理解——语法如何运作，单词在上下文中意味着什么——是普遍的。因此，代表语言流形的低维词嵌入应该可以完美地重用于这两个任务。在深度学习架构中，这意味着神经网络的早期层——作为特征提取器将复杂的原始数据映射到那个更简单的流形上——可以并且绝对应该在不同模型之间共享。你只需要专门化最后的、后面的层，以使这些共同的特征适应具体不同的任务。

    这个底层理论直接引出了迁移学习。在迁移学习中，数据类型相似，但最终任务截然不同。因为数据类型从根本上相似，我们在逻辑上假设不同的任务依赖于提取相同的低级特征。我们在实践中如何实现这一点呢？一种方法是“共享隐藏状态”。想象一个架构，其中单个输入 'x' 被输入到神经网络中。初始层是明确共享的，处理 'x' 并生成一个“共享隐藏状态”——一个基础特征的公共池。对于图像处理，这些共享层学习检测普遍的几何模式，如线条、边缘和简单的形状。从这个中央共享枢纽，网络分裂成独立的分支。任务 1 可能分支出来执行面部识别以识别一个人，而任务 2 分支出来执行情感分类以确定他们是否开心。这两个任务完全依赖于由共享早期层提取的完全相同的基础特征集。相反，你可以拥有具有多个不同输入流汇聚于一个共同输出任务的架构。例如，基于来自他们博客的文本流和来自他们视频博客的独立视频流来预测一个人的情绪。两个独立的流由专用的特征提取器处理，随后那些提取的特征被合并到一个共享的最终网络中以做出单一的预测。

    到目前为止，在现代机器学习中最普遍利用的技术是通过预训练网络进行迁移学习。该方法直截了当且极其强大。你采用一个庞大、极深的卷积神经网络，并在一个庞大的数据集上针对特定任务进行彻底的训练。一个经典的例子是在 ImageNet 数据集上训练 CNN，该数据集包含数百万张高度多样化的图像。我们称之为任务 1。因为它在如此大量的数据上接受过训练，所以这个网络被迫学习了令人难以置信的强大、广义的特征提取能力。

    现在，假设你有自己特定的、细分的问题——比如说，对显微癌细胞类型进行分类——而你只有非常小的数据集。这是任务 2。与其用随机权重初始化一个新的神经网络并试图在你的微小数据集上从头开始训练它（这几乎肯定会失败或发生大规模过拟合），不如利用预训练模型。你获取从任务 1 学到的权重，并用它们明确地“热启动”你用于任务 2 的新模型。

    在实践中，这意味着你采用预训练模型的架构，并将其学习到的权重直接复制到你的新模型中。因为预训练模型的最终输出层特定于 ImageNet 的 1000 个类别，所以你切断那最后一层，并用一个专门为你的癌细胞类别设定尺寸的新初始化的密集层替换它。当你开始在小数据集上训练时，你的模型不必花费数个 epoch 来弄清楚如何检测基本的边缘和纹理——它已经从 ImageNet 的权重中学会了。根据具体情况，你可能选择“冻结”早期卷积层的权重，这意味着你完全阻止它们在训练期间更新，并且严格只允许反向传播来调整你新的、定制的最终层。这确保了基础特征保持完美的保留，同时最后一层使它们适应新的任务。

  - [15:52:12 - 15:56:26] **Domain Adaptation and Summary**

    Finally, I will briefly touch on the theory of Domain Adaptation, which is the other major scenario in multi-task learning. Domain adaptation is required when you have an absolutely identical target task across two distinct datasets, but the underlying mathematical distribution of the input data differs drastically between the two domains.

    Consider this classic example: You want to build a model that reads the digits 0 through 9. You have the standard MNIST dataset, which is perfectly clean, standardized, grayscale, centrally aligned handwritten digits on black backgrounds. But the data you actually want to perform the task on in the real world is the SVHN dataset—the Street View House Numbers dataset. These are color photos of house numbers scraped from Google Street View; they have wildly varying fonts, severe lighting distortions, distracting backgrounds, and varying resolutions. If you train a highly accurate model purely on MNIST, and then abruptly ask it to read an SVHN image, it will fail spectacularly because the raw pixel distributions are completely alien to it. Domain adaptation seeks to solve this: if you have a model trained on the source domain (MNIST), how can you mathematically adapt it so that it performs robustly on the target domain (SVHN), specifically given that the target domain dataset is entirely unlabeled?

    We won't dive deeply into the math, but researchers have developed numerous sophisticated algorithms for domain adaptation. Some of them utilize principles we discussed in semi-supervised learning. For example, if you manage to obtain a minuscule set of labeled samples in the chaotic SVHN domain, you could use a label propagation algorithm to spread those few labels across the rest of the unlabeled SVHN dataset, utilizing a network pre-trained on MNIST as the feature extractor. Another powerful approach is to explicitly force the network to search for a common, domain-agnostic representation space. You might train a complex dual-autoencoder architecture on both MNIST and SVHN datasets simultaneously. You intentionally design the loss function such that the encoder is punished if its latent space representations contain any information that reveals which dataset the image originally came from. If successful, the model strips away the domain-specific "style" (colors, background) and extracts only the pure geometric structure of the digits. You can then comfortably train a standard classifier on this shared latent space. Finally, there is highly advanced work using optimal transport theory, where you mathematically learn optimal transport maps to literally warp and transform the probability distribution of the source domain until it perfectly matches the distribution of the target domain.

    Let's summarize the key theories we have covered today before we jump into the final coding demo. First, we explored unsupervised learning, establishing PCA as the mathematically optimal dimensionality reduction technique strictly if we are limited to linear operations. To escape those linear limitations, we introduced autoencoders, leveraging neural networks to learn highly complex, nonlinear compression and reconstruction mappings. We saw that autoencoders can be structured as undercomplete for pure dimensionality reduction, overcomplete (with regularization) for feature extraction, or specifically trained to perform denoising. We then moved to semi-supervised learning, addressing the reality of scarce labeled data. We detailed how algorithms like self-training and label propagation rely heavily on fundamental assumptions—like continuity, clusters, and manifolds—to extract predictive power from unlabeled datasets. Finally, we surveyed multitask learning, highlighting transfer learning and domain adaptation. The unifying, overarching theme connecting every single topic today is the concept of representation learning. In all these cases, whether using PCA, autoencoders, or a pre-trained CNN, the fundamental goal is not just mapping inputs to outputs, but intelligently learning an underlying, useful, and robust structural representation of the raw data within a latent space.

    We will take another quick break now. When we return, we will jump into the Jupyter notebooks and explicitly code up the transfer learning implementation.

    最后，我将简要探讨领域自适应的理论，这是多任务学习中另一个主要场景。当你在两个截然不同的数据集中拥有绝对相同的目标任务，但输入数据的潜在数学分布在两个领域之间存在巨大差异时，就需要进行领域自适应。

    考虑这个经典的例子：你想构建一个读取数字 0 到 9 的模型。你有标准的 MNIST 数据集，这是完美的、干净的、标准化的、居中对齐的、黑色背景上的灰度手写数字。但你在现实世界中真正想对其执行任务的数据是 SVHN 数据集——街景门牌号数据集。这些是从谷歌街景抓取的门牌号彩色照片；它们有极其多样的字体、严重的照明失真、分散注意力的背景和不同的分辨率。如果你纯粹在 MNIST 上训练一个高度准确的模型，然后突然让它读取 SVHN 图像，它将惨败，因为原始像素分布对它来说完全是陌生的。领域自适应试图解决这个问题：如果你有一个在源领域（MNIST）上训练的模型，你如何在数学上对其进行调整，使其在目标领域（SVHN）上健壮地运行，特别是在目标领域数据集完全未标记的情况下？

    我们不会深入研究数学，但研究人员已经为领域自适应开发了大量复杂的算法。其中一些利用了我们在半监督学习中讨论过的原则。例如，如果你设法在混乱的 SVHN 领域获得极少量的标记样本，你可以使用标签传播算法将那几个标签散布到未标记 SVHN 数据集的其余部分，利用在 MNIST 上预训练的网络作为特征提取器。另一种强大的方法是明确强制网络寻找一个通用的、与领域无关的表示空间。你可能会同时在 MNIST 和 SVHN 数据集上训练一个复杂的双自编码器架构。你有意设计损失函数，使得如果编码器的潜在空间表示包含任何揭示图像最初来自哪个数据集的信息，它就会受到惩罚。如果成功，该模型会剥离特定领域的“风格”（颜色、背景），只提取数字纯粹的几何结构。然后，你就可以安心地在这个共享的潜在空间上训练一个标准分类器。最后，还有使用最优传输理论的非常高级的工作，在那里你通过数学学习最优传输图，从字面上扭曲和变换源领域的概率分布，直到它完美匹配目标领域的分布。

    在进入最后的编码演示之前，让我们总结一下今天涵盖的关键理论。首先，我们探讨了无监督学习，确立了如果我们仅限于线性操作，PCA 是数学上最佳的降维技术。为了摆脱这些线性限制，我们引入了自编码器，利用神经网络学习高度复杂的、非线性的压缩和重构映射。我们看到自编码器可以被结构化为不完备的（用于纯粹的降维）、过完备的（带有正则化，用于特征提取），或专门训练来执行去噪。然后我们转向半监督学习，解决标记数据稀缺的现实。我们详细说明了像自训练和标签传播这样的算法是如何严重依赖基本假设——如连续性、聚类和流形——从未标记数据集中提取预测能力的。最后，我们考察了多任务学习，重点强调了迁移学习和领域自适应。连接今天每一个主题的统一、总体的主题是表示学习的概念。在所有这些情况下，无论是使用 PCA、自编码器还是预训练的 CNN，根本目标不仅仅是将输入映射到输出，而是智能地在潜在空间内学习原始数据的底层的、有用的且健壮的结构表示。

For this demo, we will be using the CIFAR-10 dataset, which you might recall we used previously when discussing adversarial training. Remember, ImageNet is a vastly larger and more complex dataset than CIFAR-10, containing thousands of different object classes compared to CIFAR-10's ten.     作为理论部分的简短总结，请记住，本课程旨在为你们提供对深度学习的全面理解，深度学习是一项关键的机器学习技术，在人工智能和数据科学领域有着广泛的应用。现在，让我们看看今天的实践演示，了解如何在代码中实现迁移学习。在这个演示中，我们将使用 CIFAR-10 数据集，你们可能还记得我们在讨论对抗性训练时使用过它。简单回顾一下，CIFAR-10 由分为十个不同类别的标准 RGB 彩色图像组成。在这个练习中，我们将利用一个预训练的卷积神经网络 (CNN)，并以两种截然不同的方式应用它：第一，仅仅使用预训练的权重作为我们模型参数的初始化器以提供热启动；第二，通过完全冻结其权重，将网络用作固定的特征提取器。在现实世界的实践中，只要有可能，采用这些非常成功、成熟的模型之一，并仅仅对架构进行微调以适应你的特定任务，几乎总是比从头开始构建要好得多。

    We start by importing the ResNet50 model directly from our library, and we will set this up as our initial baseline model. When instantiating this model, there are several important arguments we need to specify. One key argument is `include_top`. What does this mean? The "top" layer refers to the final dense, fully connected layers that come right after all the convolutional layers. In our baseline setup, we set this to true to include those top layers, meaning we are loading the full, complete model architecture. Another crucial argument is `weights`. For our initial baseline, which we can call our "out-of-the-can" model that is ready to use, we set `weights=None`. This explicitly tells the framework not to use the pre-trained ImageNet weights; instead, it will initialize all the millions of parameters with completely random values. We also need to specify the `input_shape` argument. Since our dataset is CIFAR-10, we define the input shape as 32 by 32 by 3, and we set our final output to accommodate 10 different classes, replacing the default ImageNet output parameters.

    When we print out the model summary, you will immediately notice that there are a tremendous number of layers. The types of layers are exactly what we expect from a CNN: you have your standard convolution layers, batch normalization layers, and pooling layers repeated extensively throughout the network. It's a very deep structure, so there isn't too much to point out layer-by-layer right now, other than acknowledging its sheer size. The convolutions eventually end, and then you hit an average pooling layer. This average pooling operation is actually quite crude: it takes the entire remaining spatial feature map and simply averages all the values down into a single pixel representation. This is followed by the dense top layers to ensure everything outputs to the correct final dimension. Overall, this baseline ResNet50 model contains roughly 23 million parameters. We set up our standard training callbacks to save the model and define our specific keyword arguments for the fitting process. We then proceed to train this randomly initialized baseline model for just 20 epochs. Looking at the output, there really isn't much to say other than the fact that it clearly has not converged. Because we randomly initialized a massive 23-million-parameter model, it requires a significant amount of time and many more epochs to properly train from scratch. Since it takes so long to train such a big network, we do not actually want to keep training this baseline.

    我们首先直接从库中导入 ResNet50 模型，并将其设置为我们最初的基线模型。在实例化这个模型时，我们需要指定几个重要的参数。一个关键的参数是 `include_top`。这是什么意思？“顶”层指的是紧跟在所有卷积层之后的最终的密集全连接层。在我们的基线设置中，我们将其设置为 true 以包含这些顶层，这意味着我们正在加载完整、全部的模型架构。另一个关键参数是 `weights`。对于我们最初的基线，我们可以称之为准备就绪的“开箱即用”模型，我们设置 `weights=None`。这明确地告诉框架不要使用预训练的 ImageNet 权重；相反，它将用完全随机的值初始化所有数百万个参数。我们还需要指定 `input_shape` 参数。由于我们的数据集是 CIFAR-10，我们将输入形状定义为 32 乘 32 乘 3，并将我们的最终输出设置为容纳 10 个不同的类别，以取代默认的 ImageNet 输出参数。

    当我们打印出模型摘要时，你会立即注意到有数量惊人的层。层的类型正是我们对 CNN 的期望：你有标准的卷积层、批量归一化层和池化层在整个网络中广泛重复。这是一个非常深的结构，所以除了承认它庞大的规模之外，现在没有太多需要逐层指出的。卷积最终结束，然后你遇到一个平均池化层。这种平均池化操作实际上相当粗糙：它提取整个剩余的空间特征图，并简单地将所有值平均成一个单像素表示。紧随其后的是密集的顶层，以确保所有输出都符合正确的最终维度。总的来说，这个基线 ResNet50 模型包含大约 2300 万个参数。我们设置了标准的训练回调来保存模型，并定义了用于拟合过程的特定关键字参数。然后，我们将这个随机初始化的基线模型仅仅训练了 20 个 epoch。看看输出，除了它显然没有收敛这一事实之外，真的没什么好说的。因为我们随机初始化了一个庞大的 2300 万参数的模型，它需要大量的时间和多得多的 epoch 才能从头开始正确地训练。由于训练如此庞大的网络需要很长时间，我们实际上并不想继续训练这个基线模型。

  - [16:11:02 - 16:13:40] **Fine-Tuning with Pre-Trained ImageNet Weights**

    Instead of wasting time training from scratch, we will employ transfer learning by utilizing pre-trained weights. The way to do this is exceptionally simple. Whereas previously we defined `weights=None`, we now change that code to `weights='imagenet'`. This single change commands the framework to load the optimal weights that were learned when this exact model was exhaustively trained on the ImageNet dataset. Furthermore, we make another critical architectural change: we set `include_top=False`. Why do we do this? Because the original top layer of this pre-trained model was specifically engineered and sized to output 1000 different classes for ImageNet. That specific dense layer is entirely useless for our CIFAR-10 task, which only has 10 classes. By omitting the top layer, we effectively truncate the network, keeping only the powerful feature-extracting convolutional layers and their pre-trained weights.

    Because we removed the original top layer, we must manually custom-build and append our own dense layers at the end of the network to correctly output 10 classes. When you review the model summary now, it looks identical to the baseline, except it cleanly ends at the final convolution, completely lacking those dense layers until we add them by hand. Now, we proceed to train this newly configured model, using the pre-trained weights as our warm-start initializer. Looking at the training curves, the first thing that becomes abundantly clear is that convergence is vastly faster compared to the random initialization. The model already knows how to extract rich visual features, so it learns the new task rapidly. However, another phenomenon is also glaringly obvious: there is a massive gap between the test set accuracy and the training set accuracy. The model is severely overfitting. This occurs because the ResNet architecture is simply too huge—23 million parameters—relative to our relatively small CIFAR-10 training set of only 50,000 images. The model has so much capacity that it easily memorizes the training data. So, while pre-training gave us rapid convergence, we now have a massive overfitting problem to solve.

    与其浪费时间从头开始训练，我们不如利用预训练的权重来实施迁移学习。这样做的方法非常简单。之前我们定义了 `weights=None`，现在我们将该代码更改为 `weights='imagenet'`。这一个简单的更改指示框架加载当这个完全相同的模型在 ImageNet 数据集上进行详尽训练时学到的最佳权重。此外，我们进行了另一个关键的架构更改：我们设置了 `include_top=False`。我们为什么要这样做？因为这个预训练模型最初的顶层是专门设计和调整大小以为 ImageNet 输出 1000 个不同的类别的。那个特定的密集层对于我们只有 10 个类别的 CIFAR-10 任务来说完全没用。通过省略顶层，我们实际上截断了网络，只保留了强大的提取特征的卷积层及其预训练的权重。

    因为我们移除了原始的顶层，我们必须在网络的末端手动定制并附加我们自己的密集层，以正确地输出 10 个类别。当你现在查看模型摘要时，它看起来与基线相同，只是它干净利落地在最后的卷积处结束，完全没有那些密集层，直到我们手动添加它们。现在，我们继续训练这个新配置的模型，使用预训练的权重作为我们的热启动初始化器。看看训练曲线，第一件变得非常清楚的事情是，与随机初始化相比，收敛速度要快得多。模型已经知道如何提取丰富的视觉特征，所以它能迅速学会新任务。然而，另一个现象也极其明显：测试集准确率和训练集准确率之间存在巨大的差距。模型正在严重过拟合。发生这种情况是因为，相对于我们只有 50,000 张图像的相对较小的 CIFAR-10 训练集来说，ResNet 架构实在太庞大了——2300 万个参数。模型具有如此大的容量，以至于它轻松记住了训练数据。所以，虽然预训练为我们带来了快速的收敛，但我们现在必须解决一个大规模的过拟合问题。

  - [16:13:40 - 16:18:24] **Freezing Weights for Feature Extraction and Course Admin**

    How can we mitigate this severe overfitting? We could certainly apply the standard regularization techniques we learned earlier, which would definitely help here. But the most obvious root problem is that the trainable model is simply way too big for our dataset. The strategy we will employ here is to fix, or freeze, a significant portion of the network's weights. In doing this, we are making the fundamental assumption that the low-level visual features learned from ImageNet are universally applicable, meaning both CIFAR-10 and ImageNet can rely on those exact same fixed feature extractors for a lot of their convolutional layers. By freezing these layers, we will drastically reduce the total number of trainable parameters, which directly reduces the complexity of the model and heavily combats overfitting.

    To freeze the weights in Keras, you can either set the entire model's trainable flag to false (`model.trainable = False`), or you can do it iteratively layer by layer (`layer.trainable = False`). We will use the layer-by-layer approach. We will loop through the network and freeze all the convolutional weights, making them non-trainable. However, we intentionally write a line of code to ensure that the Batch Normalization layers specifically remain trainable. Why? The batch normalization layers contain moving averages that were calculated explicitly with respect to the ImageNet dataset. It does not make mathematical sense to apply ImageNet's statistical moving averages to our CIFAR-10 images because the underlying data domains are different. So, we freeze the convolutions but keep the batch norm layers actively updating their statistics to fit our new data.

    After setting this up, we append our custom top dense layers just as before. Now let's analyze the model summary. We have this massive ResNet50 architecture, but we've selectively disabled training for the bulk of it. The top dense layer we added introduces about 20,000 trainable parameters. The batch normalization layers that we kept active contribute roughly another 53,000 parameters. So, out of a monolithic 23-million-parameter network, we are now only actively training about 73,000 parameters. We train this frozen-weight model, and looking at the resulting performance graph, the overfitting problem has been successfully eliminated. It does still take some time to converge fully, so you would likely want to train it for more epochs, but the crippling variance is gone. To improve this even further in the future, you could experiment with selective unfreezing—perhaps allowing the later, more specialized convolution layers to remain trainable while strictly freezing the early ones. You can also combine this with all the regularization techniques and data augmentation methods we have learned.

    Are there any final questions regarding this implementation? Okay, if not, that concludes our material for today. Just a few administrative notes: there is no lecture next week. For the week after that, which is the 28th, please make sure you have Examsoft installed and everything set up on your personal laptops before the next lecture so that you can get the appropriate help on the day if needed. Following this, our next major topic will be generative models, which will likely take up the subsequent two or three lectures. Have a great week, and see you next time.

    我们如何减轻这种严重的过拟合呢？我们当然可以应用我们之前学过的标准正则化技术，这绝对会有所帮助。但最明显的根本问题是，对于我们的数据集来说，可训练的模型实在太大了。我们将在这里采用的策略是固定或冻结网络中很大一部分的权重。在这样做时，我们做出了一个基本假设，即从 ImageNet 学到的低级视觉特征是普遍适用的，这意味着 CIFAR-10 和 ImageNet 在其许多卷积层中都可以依赖完全相同的固定特征提取器。通过冻结这些层，我们将大幅减少可训练参数的总数，这直接降低了模型的复杂性并有力地对抗了过拟合。

    为了在 Keras 中冻结权重，你要么可以将整个模型的可训练标志设置为 false (`model.trainable = False`)，要么可以逐层迭代地进行 (`layer.trainable = False`)。我们将使用逐层的方法。我们将遍历网络并冻结所有的卷积权重，使它们不可训练。然而，我们有意写了一行代码，以确保批量归一化 (Batch Normalization) 层专门保持可训练状态。为什么？批量归一化层包含显式基于 ImageNet 数据集计算出的移动平均值。将 ImageNet 的统计移动平均值应用于我们的 CIFAR-10 图像在数学上是没有意义的，因为底层的数据领域是不同的。因此，我们冻结卷积，但保持批量归一化层积极地更新其统计数据以适应我们的新数据。

    设置好之后，我们像以前一样附加我们定制的顶部密集层。现在让我们分析一下模型摘要。我们拥有这个庞大的 ResNet50 架构，但我们有选择地禁用了其中大部分的训练。我们添加的顶部密集层引入了大约 20,000 个可训练参数。我们保持活跃的批量归一化层贡献了大约另外 53,000 个参数。因此，在一个由 2300 万个参数组成的庞大网络中，我们现在只积极训练大约 73,000 个参数。我们训练这个冻结权重的模型，看看结果性能图，过拟合问题已被成功消除。它确实仍然需要一些时间来完全收敛，所以你可能需要对它进行更多 epoch 的训练，但那种破坏性的方差已经消失了。为了在将来进一步改进这一点，你可以尝试选择性地解冻——也许允许后面更专业的卷积层保持可训练状态，同时严格冻结早期的卷积层。你也可以将此与我们学过的所有正则化技术和数据增强方法结合起来。

    关于这个实现还有什么最后的问题吗？好的，如果没有，我们今天的内容就到此结束。只有几点行政上的注意事项：下周没有讲座。在下下周，也就是 28 号，请确保在下一次讲座之前在您的个人笔记本电脑上安装了 Examsoft 并设置好了一切，以便如果需要，您可以在当天获得适当的帮助。在此之后，我们的下一个主要主题将是生成模型，这可能会占用随后的两到三次讲座。祝大家度过愉快的一周，下次见。
## Week 9
  - [14:00:00 - 14:26:44] **Course Administration and Review of Previous Concepts**

    Welcome everyone to Lecture 9 of DSA 5204, Deep Learning and Applications. Let's go ahead and get started. Over the first part of this session, I want us to settle in and do a comprehensive review of what we covered last week before we dive into today's entirely new topic. Previously, we spent a lot of time discussing various unsupervised and supervised learning algorithms. A major theme of our recent lectures has been figuring out how to overcome the common problem of missing labels in our datasets.   - [14:26:44 - 14:31:46] **Recap: Semi-Supervised Learning and Introduction to Generative Models**

    To briefly continue our recap from last week, we looked beyond standard supervised learning. We explored unsupervised learning as well as semi-supervised learning. Semi-supervised learning simply means that you have both labeled and unlabeled data—typically a much larger quantity of unlabeled data—and you want to somehow make use of those unlabeled samples in a prediction task. We saw that as long as you can make certain mathematical assumptions, such as the continuity clustering assumption or the manifold assumption, the unlabeled data can actually be incredibly helpful for your predictions. The main concept that transitions us from last week to this week is the autoencoder. We initially motivated autoencoders from the point of view of unsupervised learning, specifically for non-linear dimensionality reduction. We already knew about Principal Component Analysis (PCA), which is the optimal linear dimensionality reduction tool if your sole goal is to minimize reconstruction loss. However, we saw through various image examples that linear reduction is often not good enough. We needed a non-linear version, and that is precisely what autoencoders provide. You use a neural network as an encoder to reduce the high-dimensional input data down to a lower-dimensional representation in a latent space. Then, you use another neural network as a decoder to bring that latent representation back up to reconstruct the original input data.

    Today, we will be looking at how we can leverage the concept of autoencoders to help us generate entirely new images. That is the core task: generating new samples given a dataset of existing samples. One fundamental tool to achieve this is to learn the underlying distribution from which the data was originally drawn. Let's look at generative models from a big-picture perspective first. In machine learning, we often divide models into two broad categories: discriminative models and generative models. You can think of discriminative models as trying to learn a mathematical decision boundary to effectively separate two or more classes of objects. Examples of this include standard image classification or sentiment analysis in text. On the other hand, generative models have a different end goal. Their ultimate objective is to generate completely new, synthetic samples of data. To do this successfully, a critical step is modeling and learning the underlying probability distribution, p*, where the original data was drawn from.

    This leads us to the concept of density estimation. The mathematical setting is that you are given a dataset consisting of N data points, denoted as x_i, where each x_i is drawn independent and identically distributed from some underlying, true distribution p*. The goal of density estimation is to learn a model, let's call it p_hat, that is as close to the underlying distribution p* as possible. Generative modeling shares this exact same setting and given data, but the explicit goal is to learn a model that allows you to easily sample new data points, x_tilde, that look incredibly realistic, as if they naturally came from the true distribution p*. At first glance, density estimation and generative modeling seem like they might be the exact same problem, and practically speaking, sometimes they are. However, a crucial requirement for generative models is that whatever model you ultimately learn must allow for efficient sampling. If you look at this purely from a density estimation standpoint, you might design an extremely complex model p_hat that perfectly describes the density but is computationally agonizing to do inference on. If it is too slow to sample from, it fails as a practical generative model. Furthermore, some density estimators might not possess specific properties we want, such as ensuring a wide diversity in the generated images.
